The complete Kaluza–Klein spectra of 𝓝 = 𝟏 \bm{\mathcal{N}=1} and 𝓝 = 𝟎 \bm{\mathcal{N}=0} M-theory on 𝑨 ​ 𝒅 ​ 𝑺 𝟒 × ( squashed ​ 𝑺 𝟕 ) \bm{AdS_{4}\times(\text{squashed}\ S^{7})}
Joel Karlsson, and Bengt E.W. Nilsson

Abstract
The squashed seven-sphere operator spectrum is completed by deriving the spectrum of the spin-3/2 operator. The implications of the results for the A ​ d ​ S 4 AdS_{4} 𝒩 = 1 \mathcal{N}=1 supermultiplets obtained from compactification of eleven-dimensional supergravity are analysed. The weak G 2 G_{2} holonomy plays an important role when solving the eigenvalue equations on the squashed sphere. Here, a novel and more universal algebraic approach to the whole eigenvalue problem on coset manifolds is provided. Having obtained full control of all the operator spectra, we can finally determine the irreps D ⁡ ( E 0 , s ) D(E_{0},s) for all supermultiplets in the left-squashed vacuum. This includes an analysis of possible boundary conditions. By performing an orientation flip on the seven-sphere, we also obtain the full spectrum for the non-supersymmetric right-squashed compactification which is of interest in the swampland context and in particular for the A ​ d ​ S AdS swampland conjecture. Here, a number of boundary condition ambiguities arise making the analysis of dual marginal operators somewhat involved. This work is a direct continuation of Nilsson:2018lof and Ekhammar:2021gsg .



1
Introduction

Recently there has appeared a number of papers discussing various non-supersymmetric
AdS
flux-solutions in ten- and
eleven-dimensional supergravity aiming at determining their stability properties, perturbatively as well as non-perturbatively.
The interest in this issue is a direct result of the so called swampland program
Vafa:2005ui
;
Ooguri:2006in

and in particular the “
AdS
swampland conjecture”
Ooguri:2016pdq
;
Freivogel:2016qwc
claiming that any
AdS
flux-compactification without supersymmetry must be unstable, either
perturbatively or, if the solution passes this test, non-perturbatively. In the former case, the Breitenlohner–Freedman (BF) stability criterion
Breitenlohner:1982jf
;
Breitenlohner:1982bm
is easily checked once
the complete linearised spectrum, or at least some crucial parts of it, is known. In the context of
AdS/CFT
,
this stability corresponds, in the infinite
N
limit of the dual field theory, to unitary conformal dimensions of single trace operators, and the vanishing of the corresponding
\beta
functions. In non-supersymmetric scenarios, there have been concerns raised in the literature (see, e.g.,
Witten:2001ua
;
Aharony:2002hx
;
Dymarsky:2005uh
;
Dymarsky:2005nc
;
Pomoni:2009joh
;
Aharony:2015afa
) regarding the
\beta
functions of marginal multi-trace operators that might be nonvanishing even if all single-trace
\beta
functions vanish
1
1

1

We are grateful to the referee for emphasising this issue and to Igor Klebanov for helpful discussions.
.
If one could find an example where this does not happen at leading order in
1/N
, there could still be subleading
1/N
effects that destroy the conformal fixed point
Berkooz:1998qp
(see also, e.g.,
Murugan:2016aty
and references therein). In the bulk, this corresponds to an instability caused by a tadpole of a composite field that destroys the BF-stable non-supersymmetric vacuum. We emphasise that the purpose of this paper is not to add to this discussion. Instead, one of the main results of our work is the possibility to choose boundary conditions (in compactifications on the squashed seven-sphere) in such a way that all marginal operators on the boundary are eliminated. This possibility was first noted in the singlet sector in
Nilsson:2023ctq
and will be explained in full detail later in this paper.

The above perturbative aspects of the stability question mean that
one essentially needs to have full control of the whole spectrum. At an even deeper level, one must discuss the possibility of instabilities due to, e.g.,
bubbles of nothing

Witten:1981gj
(see also the generalisation discussed in
Ooguri:2017njy
) and other non-perturbative issues.
Note that even if it is possible to establish stability under all known decay modes in a non-supersymmetric context, it might be very difficult, if not impossible, to produce a strict proof of stability against
all decay modes, known or not. An interesting attempt in this direction has, however, been made recently in
Giri:2021eob
.

In this line of investigation there have also been efforts to find the complete spectrum of certain compactifications that can be viewed as up-lifted stable extrema of maximal supergravity theories
in lower dimensions. With the improved knowledge of these spectra it was possible, in some cases, to pinpoint certain massive modes in the Kaluza–Klein spectrum that give rise
to so called brane-jet instabilities
Bena:2020xxb
.

In the same vein, there are also some special non-supersymmetric BF-stable solutions that have been discussed recently.
These include the interesting case of the non-supersymmetric
G_{2}
solution first found in massive type II supergravity, see, e.g.,
Lust:2008zd
;
Borghese:2012qm
.
2
2

2

For some more recent extensive searches for vacuum solutions, see, e.g.,
Bobev:2019dik
;
Bobev:2020qev
.

This case seemed for a long time to clash with the
AdS

swampland conjecture
Guarino:2020jwv
;
Guarino:2020flh
but was
recently
Bomans:2021ara
;
Dibitetto:2021ltm
shown to be afflicted by the non-perturbative phenomenon of nucleation of a bubble of nothing first described by Witten
Witten:1981gj
.
Another attempt to find a solution of type IIB supergravity in
D=10
that violates the
AdS
swampland conjecture is analysed in
Giambrone:2021wsm
based on a non-geometric S-fold construction. This case is further
discussed in the review
Guarino:2022tlw
, where its chances of surviving all known decay modes is commented upon. It is probably fair to say that its full stability is far from being demonstrated
although it does possess some novel and quite interesting features.

At the point in time when this is written all known non-supersymmetric
AdS
flux-compactifications have been shown to be unstable (with a slight caveat for the last case mentioned above) except for the skew-whiffed
squashed seven-sphere solution discussed in
Duff:1986hr
. The reason this solution of eleven-dimensional supergravity has not been fully analysed until now is that the results in
Nilsson:2018lof
;
Ekhammar:2021gsg
were still lacking the spin-3/2 eigenvalues on the squashed seven-sphere and that the methods used in
Malek:2019eaz
;
Malek:2020mlk
have not been applicable to
vacua that are not lifts from

\mathcal{N}=8
supergravity in four dimensions. However, with the present work and the recent results of
Duboeuf:2022mam
, the shortcomings in both these cases have been overcome.
For further comments on the relation between our work and
Duboeuf:2022mam
, see the end of this introduction.

In this paper we finish the work started in
Nilsson:2018lof
;
Ekhammar:2021gsg
by completing the determination of the spectrum in the sense that we have finally
solved also the spin-3/2 equation and thus we have now determined the spectra of all relevant eigenvalue equations on the squashed seven-sphere. This result has already been reported on in the
MSc thesis
Karlsson:2021oxd
by one of the present authors.
Applying this to the left-squashed case, we then provide a unique assignment of
masses and energies to the
\mathcal{N}=1
supermultiplets, up to some ambiguities related to boundary conditions. As we will see below, this process produces certain degeneracies in the spectrum. We will, towards the end of the
paper, discuss this further without providing a clear answer as to why this happens. This spectrum degeneracy is apparent both from the supermultiplet analysis and the explicit construction of the two-form mode functions on the squashed seven-sphere.

The stability issue of the non-supersymmetric right-squashed seven-sphere can now be addressed beyond its BF stability

Duff:1984sv
;
Duff:1983ajq
. Here, we analyse in detail which marginal operators can appear that might lead to the kind of perturbative instability mentioned above.
These latter results generalise those for the singlet sector obtained recently in
Nilsson:2023ctq
.

This paper is organised as follows. In
Section

2
, we give some background to the whole problem of finding the
spectrum of eleven-dimensional supergravity compactified on the squashed seven-sphere, following
Duff:1986hr
, and
present new universal versions (derived in the MSc thesis
Karlsson:2021oxd
) of some of the key coset space formulas used in
Duff:1986hr
and particularly in the
G_{2}
approach of
Ekhammar:2021gsg
. This involves
obtaining a more powerful group theoretic expression for the action of the Weyl tensor on the various isotropy irreps appearing in the spectrum analysis.

In
Section

3
, we rederive the operator spectra previously derived in
Duff:1986hr
and
Ekhammar:2021gsg
and complete this by
obtaining also the so far missing spectrum, namely the spin-3/2 one, following
Karlsson:2021oxd
.
Then, in
Section

4
, all two-form modes are constructed explicitly and their eigenvalues derived with some intriguing implications.
In
Section

5
, we use the now complete information on the operator eigenvalues on the squashed seven-sphere to give masses and
E_{0}
values to all the supermultiplets.
We find here that supersymmetry does not prevent a certain amount of ambiguities related to the possibility to choose different boundary conditions in
AdS_{4}
.
These features are also analysed for the skew-whiffed theory without supersymmetry which has a rather large number of fields with undetermined boundary conditions. This section ends
with a discussion on marginal operators and the implications for the issue of stability.
The eigenvalue degeneracy problem that arose in previous sections is addressed. Finally, the situation is summarised and commented upon in the concluding
Section

6
.

Some of the results in this paper, in particular the improved methods for computing the
eigenvalues of differential operators on coset spaces reported on in
Section

2
and the eigenvalues of the spin-3/2 operator on the Einstein-squashed seven-sphere
derived in
Section

3
, have already appeared in the MSc thesis
Karlsson:2021oxd
.
A preliminary study of how the possible values of
E_{0}
fit into supermultiplets was also conducted in
Karlsson:2021oxd
.
Here, we complete this analysis by also studying the special cases that arise for non-generic isometry irreps.

During the final stages of this project, there appeared another paper
Duboeuf:2022mam
, obtaining results on the spectrum of
E_{0}
for low-lying levels in the bosonic sector of the squashed
S^{7}
theory discussed here.
These results, derived in
Duboeuf:2022mam
by entirely different methods from the ones presented here, coincide with ours where they can be compared.

Duboeuf:2022mam
provides one piece of information not yet obtained by the methods in this paper, namely the connection between
E_{0}
values and irreps of the Wess–Zumino multiplets which contain scalar fields related to the Lichnerowicz operator on the squashed
S^{7}
.
This helps us resolve a final ambiguity in our work related to the assignment of multiplicities for these particular Wess–Zumino multiplets, as explained in
Section

5
(see e.g.
Figure

5
).
This information could have been obtained by repeating the two-form eigenmode construction in
Section

4
for the metric modes on the squashed
S^{7}
, which has not yet been done.
In contrast to
Duboeuf:2022mam
, our methods automatically provide the entire infinite towers in the spectra and we verify the presence of supersymmetry for all supermultiplets with maximum spin
2
,
3/2
and
1
by deriving the fermionic spectra independently.
Furthermore, we explicitly construct the complete infinite towers of two-form Laplacian eigenmodes on the squashed
S^{7}
for the first time.
We also analyse the role of boundary conditions and find that there are multiple choices that respect supersymmetry in the left-squashed vacuum (see
Table

5
).
A limitation of our methods is that they rely on a coset description of the internal manifold which does not seem to be the case for the methods used in
Duboeuf:2022mam
.

2
The squashed
S^{7}
eigenvalue problem

To make this paper relatively self-contained we will in the first subsection reproduce a few tables from
previous works, the review
Duff:1986hr
and the more recent
Nilsson:2018lof
and
Ekhammar:2021gsg
.
These tables contain information of general validity and provide useful information for the discussions in this paper,
for instance how many supermultiplets of each kind that appear in the

\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric
AdS_{4}
vacuum of eleven-dimensional supergravity on the left-squashed seven-sphere

Nilsson:2018lof
.
One reason for studying the supersymmetric left-squashed case first is to be able to
establish the correct operator eigenvalue spectra which would be a much more intricate task without the possibility to check the results using supersymmetry.
Having done this, we can then perform a skew-whiffing (i.e., an orientation flip) on the squashed seven-sphere to get the complete field spectrum in the non-supersymmetric
right-squashed case for which the
AdS_{4}
stability issue will be analysed in later sections.

In the second subsection we then briefly describe our method, as used in the literature so far, to derive the eigenvalue spectra of the operators on the squashed seven-sphere appearing in the compactification

Duff:1986hr
;
Ekhammar:2021gsg
. The main purpose here is to present an improved formalism by deriving new more general and powerful versions of some of the coset space formulas,
the implications of which are presented in the next section, largely following
Karlsson:2021oxd
.

Review

\mathcal{N}=1

With the information in
Tables

2
and
3
at hand, we can start discussing how to obtain the various spectra of the operators on the squashed seven-sphere.
The spectrum of
\Delta_{0}
and the Dirac operator were derived for general squashing in
Nilsson:1983ru
. While that derivation is a bit different from the one we present in
Section

3
, it essentially coincides with
the one given in
Section

4
.
The result for the square of the Dirac operator obtained in
Section

3
was derived in
Ekhammar:2021gsg
but then only for the squashed Einstein case, in contrast to
Nilsson:1983ru
.

Turning to the Hodge–de Rham and Lichnerowicz operators, we note that the former ones are positive definite and defined by

\Delta_{p}=\delta d+d\delta,

(7)

whereas the latter, which is not manifestly positive definite, acts on transverse and traceless metric modes and is defined by

\Delta_{L}h_{ab}=-\Box h_{ab}-2R_{a}{}^{c}{}_{b}{}^{d}h_{cd}+2R_{(a}{}^{c}h_{b)c}=-\Box h_{ab}-2W_{a}{}^{c}{}_{b}{}^{d}h_{cd}+14m^{2}h_{ab}.

(8)

In the last expression, the presence of the Weyl tensor
W_{abcd}
gives rise to complications when solving
the eigenvalue equations. This problem was addressed in
Ekhammar:2021gsg
case by case and will in this paper be discussed again in the next subsection
where we derive a universal group theoretic formula for the Weyl tensor eigenvalues reducing the work needed and providing a much better understanding of
the whole issue.

2.2
Improved methodology

We now turn to the improved formalism that will facilitate the eigenvalue analysis.
This subsection follows
Karlsson:2021oxd
, where some additional details can be found.

Since we are interested in eigenvalue equations involving the Laplacian we start from its standard expression
\Delta=d\delta+\delta d
and rewrite it as

\Delta\equiv-\Box-[D_{a},D_{b}]\Sigma^{ab}=-\Box-R_{abcd}\Sigma^{ab}\Sigma^{cd},

(9)

which we will refer to as “the universal Laplacian” since it cannot only act on
p
-forms but any tensor field. Here and below,
\Sigma^{ab}
are the
Spin(7)
generators. To see how the right-hand side arises just consider the following
p
-form identities

\displaystyle\Delta_{p}\alpha_{a_{1}...a_{p}}=(d\delta+\delta d)\alpha_{a_{1}...a_{p}}=-\Box\alpha_{a_{1}...a_{p}}-p[D_{[a_{1}},D_{|b|}]\alpha^{b}{}_{a_{2}...a_{p}]}

\displaystyle=-\Box\alpha_{a_{1}...a_{p}}-[D_{c_{1}},D_{c_{2}}]p\delta^{c_{1}c_{2}}_{[a_{1}|b|}\alpha^{b}{}_{a_{2}...a_{p}]}=-\Box\alpha_{a_{1}...a_{p}}-[D_{c_{1}},D_{c_{2}}]\Sigma^{c_{1}c_{2}}\alpha_{a_{1}...a_{p}}.

(10)

This operator is the relevant one for transverse
p
-forms and becomes the Lichnerowicz operator when acting on traceless and transverse
second rank symmetric tensors. It also appears when squaring the Dirac operator acting on spinors as well as on transverse and gamma-traceless vector-spinors. In fact,

(i\not{D})^{2}\psi=\Delta\psi+\frac{21}{4}m^{2}\psi,\qquad\quad(i\not{D})^{2}\psi_{a}=\Delta\psi_{a}-\frac{3}{4}m^{2}\psi_{a},

(11)

for Einstein spaces with
R_{ab}=6m^{2}\delta_{ab}
.

The next ingredient we need is the coset structure
G/H
of the internal manifold on which we perform the compactification. As usual
we will use a reductive coset which means that the structure constants satisfy (see, e.g.,
Duff:1986hr
)

[H,H]=H,\qquad[H,T]=T,\qquad[T,T]=H\oplus T.

(12)

We use the index split
A=(a,i)
where
A
and
i
are adjoint indices of
G
and
H
, respectively, while
a
is
a vector index in the tangent space of the coset (
a=1,2,...,dim(G)-dim(H)
). Hence, the independent non-zero structure constants are

f_{AB}{}^{C}=(f_{ij}{}^{k},f_{ia}{}^{b},f_{ab}{}^{i},f_{ab}{}^{c}).

(13)

It is then straightforward to show that the Riemann tensor of the coset manifold
G/H
can be expressed in terms of the structure constants in the following
way
Bais:1983wc
:

R_{cda}{}^{b}=f_{cd}{}^{i}f_{ia}{}^{b}+\frac{1}{2}f_{cd}{}^{e}f_{ea}{}^{b}+\frac{1}{2}f_{[c|a|}{}^{e}f_{d]e}{}^{b}.

(14)

Usually, one lowers and raises indices on the structure constants using the Killing form and its inverse.
In this paper, we do
not
do this.
Instead, we assume (already in
Equation

14
) that the coset is normal homogeneous, i.e., that there exists a
G
-invariant
g_{AB}
which is block-diagonal over
H\oplus T
with blocks
\delta_{ab}
and
g_{ij}
and use these and their inverses to raise and lower indices.
5
5

5

This assumption is not strictly necessary here, see
Karlsson:2021oxd
, but will be crucial later. The squashed seven-sphere is normal homogeneous.

The key equation needed to compute spectra of operators on the coset is the so called
coset master equation
which expresses the
H
-covariant derivative algebraically in terms of the
G
generators in the tangent space directions
T_{a}

Salam:1981xd
;
Duff:1986hr
:

D_{a}Y+\frac{1}{2}f_{abc}\Sigma^{bc}Y=-T_{a}Y.

(15)

This is a schematic equation, see
Equation

18
for a more precise version.
The reason why this is not precise is that the left-hand side is well-defined only for a
Spin(7)
tensor field
Y
while the right-hand side requires a field
Y
transforming in a representation of
G
.
The equation is, however, still very useful since both
Spin(7)
and
G
representations can be decomposed into
H
-irreps.
Hence, it can be used literally with tensor fields if it is first manipulated in a way such that the operators become block-diagonal over
H
-irreps, as we will see below.

To get a clear picture of the above relation between a differential operator and algebraic quantities like group generators we consider a
G
group element representing a point on the coset manifold with coordinates
y^{a}

L_{y}=e^{y\cdot T},

(16)

where
y\cdot T
is just a sum over products of coordinates
y^{a}
and tangent space generators
T_{a}
. Then clearly the one-form
L_{y}^{-1}dL_{y}
can be expanded as follows:

L_{y}^{-1}dL_{y}=e^{a}T_{a}+\Omega^{i}T_{i},

(17)

where
d=dy^{m}\partial_{m}
,
e^{a}
are one-form vielbeins on the coset space and
\Omega^{i}
are
H
one-form gauge fields.
By harmonic analysis on the coset, fields transforming in an irrep of
H
can be expanded using
\rho(L^{-1}_{y})
, where
\rho
runs over all irreps of
G
containing the
H
-irrep
Salam:1981xd
.
The equation
Equation

17
can then be expressed as

\check{D}_{a}\rho(L^{-1}_{y})_{P}{}^{(Q)}\equiv(\partial_{a}+\Omega_{a}^{i}T_{i})\rho(L^{-1}_{y})_{P}{}^{(Q)}=-(T_{a})_{P}{}^{R}\rho(L^{-1}_{y})_{R}{}^{(Q)},

(18)

where
\check{D}_{a}
is a
H
-covariant derivative.
Note that we have here been careful and indicated with parentheses that the upper index
(Q)
is not affected either by the generators
T_{a}
or the
H
-covariant derivative
\check{D}_{a}
.
We emphasise also here that the upper
G
-irrep index in parentheses is the isometry index while the lower
G
-irrep index without parentheses should be split into
H
-irreps
so that the
H
-covariant derivative can act according to each of the
H
-irreps in the split.

However, in the present application to the squashed seven-sphere, constructed as the coset
Sp_{2}\times Sp_{1}^{C}/(Sp_{1}^{A}\times Sp_{1}^{B+C})
, the tangent space group
Spin(7)
is not the same as the isotropy group
H=Sp_{1}^{A}\times Sp_{1}^{B+C}
.
This means that in order to apply the fundamental coset equation in this case, with all eigenvalue equations expressed in terms of
Spin(7)
-covariant derivatives on the tangent space,
one has to split each
Spin(7)
-irrep of the fields on the coset into
H
-irreps, apply the fundamental coset equation and reconstruct the tangent space irreps of the fields.
This is done as follows (see
Duff:1986hr
). Acting with an exterior derivative on
Equation

17
we get a Maurer–Cartan type equation which can be divided into two equations along the different
generator directions. We find

de^{a}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{b}\wedge e^{c}f_{bc}{}^{a}-e^{b}\wedge\Omega^{i}\,f_{bi}{}^{a},\quad d\Omega^{i}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{a}\wedge e^{b}f_{ab}{}^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{j}\wedge\Omega_{k}f_{jk}{}^{i}.

(19)

Comparing the first of these equations to the definition of
\omega^{a}{}_{b}
, the torsion-free spin connection,
0=de^{a}+\omega^{a}{}_{b}\wedge e^{b}
, we find

\omega_{[bc]}{}^{a}=-\frac{1}{2}f_{bc}{}^{a}-\Omega^{i}_{[b}f_{|i|c]}{}^{a},

(20)

which implies

\omega_{abc}=-\frac{1}{2}f_{abc}-\Omega_{a}^{i}f_{ibc}.

(21)

Inserting this result into the definition of the
H
-covariant derivative,
\check{D}_{a}=\partial_{a}+\Omega_{a}^{i}T_{i}
, we see that it can be written in terms of the ordinary tangent space covariant derivative as follows

\check{D}_{a}=D_{a}+\frac{1}{2}f_{abc}\Sigma^{bc},

(22)

when acting on an
so(7)
representation.
Note that, using the
H
-covariant derivative, the coset master equation reads simply

\check{D}_{a}Y=-T_{a}Y.

(23)

We now address the point raised above, namely that we must first get an equation where the operators split over
H
-irreps before we can apply it to tensor fields.
The left-hand side has already been addressed by writing the equation using
\check{D}
instead of
D
but
T_{a}
cannot be restricted to an irrep of
H
.
Hence, we must return to the
\rho
irreps of
G
since any action of
a
T_{a}
will mix all
H
-irreps in the
G
-irrep. We then see that

T_{a}T_{b}\rho(L_{y}^{-1})=-T_{a}(\partial_{b}+\Omega^{i}_{b}T_{i})\rho(L_{y}^{-1})=-(\partial_{b}+\Omega^{i}_{b}T_{i})T_{a}\rho(L_{y}^{-1})-\Omega^{i}[T_{a},T_{i}]\rho(L_{y}^{-1}).

(24)

Using
[T_{a},T_{i}]=-f_{ia}{}^{b}T_{b}
and
Equation

18
we find that the operations are being reversed, i.e.,

T_{a}T_{b}\rho(L_{y}^{-1})=\check{D}_{b}\check{D}_{a}\rho(L_{y}^{-1}).

(25)

Note that the commutator term above is needed to get the derivative
\check{D}_{b}
to also act on the index
a
on the second derivative.
Here, we see that if we contract with
\delta^{ab}
, the operator on the left-hand side becomes the difference between two Casimirs and hence split over
H
-irreps.
Thus, since every tensor field
Y
can be split into
H
-irreducible parts and the above equation applies to each
H
-irrep separately, we get

-\check{\Box}Y=-T_{a}T^{a}Y=(C_{g}-C_{h})Y,

(26)

where
C_{g}=-g^{AB}T_{A}T_{B}
and
C_{h}=-g^{ij}T_{i}T_{j}
are Casimir operators of the two groups in the coset
G/H
.

The operator equations we aim to solve below contain the universal Laplacian
\Delta
, which in turn can be written using
\Box=D^{a}D_{a}
.
Hence, we need the explicit relation between
\Box
and
\check{\Box}
.
It can be obtained as follows: Consider first

\check{\Box}=\check{D}^{a}\big(D_{a}+\frac{1}{2}f_{abc}\Sigma^{bc}\big)=\check{D}^{a}D_{a}+\frac{1}{2}f^{ade}\Sigma_{de}\check{D}_{a},

(27)

where we used the fact that
\delta^{ab}
,
f_{abc}
and
\Sigma^{ab}
(in any irrep) are all
H
-invariant objects and hence commute with the
H
-covariant derivative

\check{D}^{a}
. Expanding also the remaining
\check{D}^{a}
operator this expression can be written

\check{\Box}={\Box}+f_{abc}\Sigma^{bc}\check{D}^{a}-\frac{1}{4}f_{abc}f^{ade}\Sigma^{bc}\Sigma_{de}.

(28)

Note that in the last term on the right-hand side the two
so(7)
generators act successively on any object to the right of them. However, the order of them is not important since the action of the
first one on the second one vanishes as is easy to check (in any irrep).

Finally, in order to obtain a useful expression for the universal Laplacian
\Delta
, whose eigenvalues we are interested in, we combine
the last equation above with
Equation

9
and
Equation

26
. The result is the following key formula

\Delta=C_{g}+f_{abc}\Sigma^{ab}\check{D}^{c}-\frac{1}{4}(3f_{abc}f^{a}{}_{de}-2f_{abd}f^{a}{}_{ce})\Sigma^{bc}\Sigma^{de}.

(29)

The next step is to introduce some explicit expressions obtained from the analysis of the squashed seven-sphere as the coset
G/H
with
G=Sp_{2}\times Sp_{1}^{C}
and
H=Sp_{1}^{A}\times Sp_{1}^{(B+C)}

where the group
Sp_{2}
has been split into
Sp_{1}^{A}\times Sp_{1}^{B}
making it possible to form the diagonal subgroup
Sp_{1}^{B+C}
appearing in
H
, see, e.g.,
Bais:1983wc
.
There is a one-parameter family of squashed seven-spheres with this isometry group but, except for the round sphere, only one is Einstein.
This is the squashed sphere we are concerned with here and it has a special role in the one-parameter family;
it is not only normal homogeneous but standard homogeneous (
g_{AB}=-\kappa_{AB}/6
)
Karlsson:2021oxd
.

The metric on the squashed seven-sphere can be written as
Duff:1986hr

ds^{2}=d\mu^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\sin^{2}{\!\mu}\,(\sigma-\tilde{\sigma})^{2}+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}\bigl((\sigma+\tilde{\sigma})+\cos{\mu}\,(\sigma-\tilde{\sigma})\bigr)^{2},

(30)

where
0\leq\mu\leq\pi
,
\lambda
is the squashing parameter and
\sigma^{i}
and
\tilde{\sigma}^{i}
are left-invariant one-forms on two copies of
SU(2)\simeq S^{3}
, satisfying
d\sigma^{i}=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k}
.
The round seven-sphere has
\lambda=1
while the Einstein-squashed one, which we focus on in what follows, has
\lambda=1/\sqrt{5}
.
Note that the above metric is dimensionless.
To make
R_{(7)}=42m^{2}
with the Einstein-squashed metric, one has to use units
m=3/(2\sqrt{5})
.
This is implicit in what follows.

From the metric, we see that an orthonormal frame is given by

e^{\hat{i}}=\frac{\lambda}{2}\bigl((\sigma^{i}+\tilde{\sigma}^{i})+\cos{\mu}\,(\sigma^{i}-\tilde{\sigma}^{i})\bigr),\qquad e^{0}=d\mu,\qquad e^{i}=\frac{1}{2}\sin{\mu}\,(\sigma^{i}-\tilde{\sigma}^{i}).

(31)

The orientation defined by this frame and the index split
a=(\hat{i},0,i)
is that of the left-squashed seven-sphere.
The right-squashed seven-sphere is obtained by flipping the orientation.
For equations that depend on the orientation, we focus on the left-squashed sphere if nothing else is specified.

One key feature of the Einstein-squashed seven-sphere is that the holonomy of the derivative entering in the Killing spinor equation is
G_{2}

Awada:1982pk
;
Duff:1986hr

and that the structure constants become related to the octonions
Bais:1983wc
via

f_{ab}{}^{c}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}a_{ab}{}^{c},

(32)

where
a_{abc}
are given by the octonionic multiplication table (see
Appendix

A
). Now we can also see one reason why the covariant derivative
\check{D}_{a}
is of interest to us:
6
6

6

The squashed seven-sphere has an
H
-structure, i.e., a reduction of the structure group
Spin(7)
to
H
, due to it being a coset
G/H
. Since
H\subset G_{2}
, it follows that the
H
-covariant derivative
\check{D}_{a}
of the
G_{2}
-invariant
a_{abc}
vanishes. The
H
-structure has torsion, see
Equation

22
,
Equation

32
, which is related to the fact that the squashed seven-sphere is only a weak
G_{2}
manifold
Joyce:2000book
.

\check{D}_{a}a_{bcd}=0.

(33)

Below we will take full advantage of this fact when expressing the eigenvalue equations we want to solve
entirely in terms of
G_{2}
quantities making the equations and the analysis of them fully
G_{2}
-covariant. The first steps in this direction were developed in
Ekhammar:2021gsg
. The usefulness of this formulation is
related to the following fact: When splitting the indices of tangent space irreps on the tensor fields into
H
-irreps it goes via
G_{2}
(as realised already in
Duff:1986hr
)

Spin(7)\quad\longrightarrow\quad G_{2}\quad\longrightarrow\quad H=Sp_{1}^{A}\times Sp_{1}^{B+C}.

(34)

As mentioned above the appearance of
G_{2}
has many implications and can be incorporated into the formalism
in a significant way as we will now show. First, consider the Weyl tensor which will appear in the eigenvalue analysis later. It can be expressed as follows:

W_{ab}{}^{cd}=R_{ab}{}^{cd}-\frac{9}{10}\delta_{ab}^{cd}=(T^{i})_{ab}(T_{i})^{cd}+\frac{1}{10}a_{ab}{}^{e}a_{e}{}^{cd}+\frac{1}{10}a_{[a}{}^{ce}a_{b]e}{}^{d}-\frac{9}{10}\delta_{ab}^{cd}.

(35)

Using the identity

a_{[a}{}^{ce}a_{b]e}{}^{d}=-\delta_{ab}^{cd}+c_{ab}{}^{cd},

(36)

we find that this can be written in terms of the
G_{2}

\bf{21}\rightarrow\bf{14}
projector
(P_{14})_{ab}{}^{cd}
as

W_{ab}{}^{cd}=(T^{i})_{ab}(T_{i})^{cd}-\frac{6}{5}(P_{14})_{ab}{}^{cd}.

(37)

Since the Weyl tensor is the source of the
G_{2}
holonomy it vanishes when contracted by
a_{abc}
over the first or last pair of indices.
Naively, one might have expected it to be proportional to the projector
P_{14}
onto the adjoint of
G_{2}
but, as we see here, there is an extra term.
Since
H
is a subgroup of
G_{2}
, this extra term is not in contradiction with the
G_{2}
holonomy.
Furthermore, since the projector
(P_{14})_{ab}{}^{cd}
actually defines
G_{2}
inside
SO(7)
we find that

W_{abcd}\Sigma^{ab}\Sigma^{cd}=\frac{6}{5}C_{g_{2}}-C_{h},

(38)

where

C_{g_{2}}=-(P_{14})_{abcd}\Sigma^{ab}\Sigma^{cd},\quad C_{h}=-T_{i}T^{i}=2C_{sp_{1}^{A}}+\frac{6}{5}C_{sp_{1}^{B+C}}.

(39)

This expression for the Weyl tensor is one of the key results of
Karlsson:2021oxd
since it eliminates in one stroke the rather cumbersome analysis to obtain the Weyl tensor eigenvalues for the various
tensors on the squashed seven-sphere performed in
Ekhammar:2021gsg
.

In the application of the master coset formula to the computation of operator eigenvalues the first step is, as done above, to square it to produce
\Delta
.
However, as we saw in
Equation

29

to actually find the eigenvalues the appearance of the operator
a_{abc}\Sigma^{ab}\check{D}^{c}
seems to require a second squaring procedure.
Inserting the octonionic information from the coset construction above into
Equation

29
we get

-\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}a_{abc}\Sigma^{ab}\check{D}^{c}=\Delta-C_{g}-\frac{6}{5}C_{so(7)}+\frac{3}{2}C_{g_{2}},

(40)

where the
g=sp_{2}\oplus sp_{1}^{C}
Casimir is (see
Section

B.2
for Casimir conventions)

C_{g}(p,q;r)=-T_{A}T^{A}=2C_{sp_{2}}(p,q)+3C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}(r).

(41)

This group theoretic expression for
a_{abc}\Sigma^{ab}\check{D}^{c}
is in the spirit of the Weyl tensor result above and constitutes the second key formula appearing in the improved formalism applied to the squashed seven-sphere.

In addition to these two key equations there is another very useful one, namely the Ricci identity for
G_{2}
derivatives

[\check{D}_{a},\check{D}_{b}]=(T^{i})_{ab}T_{i}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}a_{ab}{}^{c}\check{D}_{c}=\Bigl(W_{ab}{}^{cd}+\frac{6}{5}(P_{14})_{ab}{}^{cd}\Bigr)\Sigma_{cd}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}a_{ab}{}^{c}\check{D}_{c}.

(42)

An especially useful form of this Ricci identity is

a_{a}{}^{bc}\check{D}_{b}\check{D}_{c}=-\frac{3}{\sqrt{5}}\check{D}_{a}.

(43)

In the next section we will derive the eigenvalue spectra of all the operators relevant for the squashed seven-sphere compactification of eleven-dimensional
supergravity. The cases of
\Delta_{p}
for
p=0,1,2,3
,
\Delta_{L}
and
\not{D}_{1/2}
have been obtained before in
Ekhammar:2021gsg

(for
\Delta_{p}
with
p=1
already in
Yamagishi:1983ri
,
\not{D}_{1/2}
in
Nilsson:1983ru
and for
\Delta_{L}
in
Duff:1986hr
) but the derivations will
here be streamlined quite a bit using the novel group theoretic formulas,
Equation

38
and
Equation

40
.
In addition, with these new forms of the key equations, we can rather easily add the so far missing one (presented also in
Karlsson:2021oxd
), namely
i\not{D}_{3/2}
, to the list,
thereby making it complete. It is then possible to conduct a search for the full set of supersymmetry irreps as indicated in the previous papers on this subject

Nilsson:2018lof
and
Ekhammar:2021gsg
. This will be the goal of
Section

5
.

It might be of interest to the reader to know already here
that the supersymmetry analysis will come short of providing a full understanding of the supersymmetry multiplets: There will remain both ambiguities and
a degeneracy of eigenvalues which will require further studies. An initial result in this context will be presented in
Section

4

where we give a novel construction of all modes of the
Y_{ab}
as well as their eigenvalues.

3
Derivation of all operator eigenvalues

In this section we start, in
Section

3.1
, by briefly showing how to derive previously known results using the more powerful methods
presented in
Section

2
. Then, in
Section

3.2
we apply these methods to the novel case of spin 3/2. This case was left undone in
Ekhammar:2021gsg
but
the analysis of the spin-3/2 equation on the squashed seven-sphere, including finding its eigenvalues, have already been reported on in
Karlsson:2021oxd
.
If the reader find the following account too brief more details can be found in
Karlsson:2021oxd
.
The
master formula
used repeatedly below is
Equation

40
.

3.1
All operator equations except spin 3/2

Zero-forms

The equation to be solved in this case is

\Delta_{0}Y=\kappa^{2}_{0}Y.

(44)

The master formula
Equation

40
for
\Delta
immediately gives
(using
C_{g_{2}}=C_{so(7)}=0
)

\Delta_{0}^{(1)}=C_{g},

(45)

when applied to scalar modes.
7
7

7

We will in the following denote the eigenvalues by a superscript on the operator, as in
Equation

45
.

Note that
a_{abc}\Sigma^{ab}\check{D}^{c}Y=0
in
Equation

40
since
Y
is a scalar and the action of
\Sigma^{ab}
on the
c
-index on
\check{D}^{c}
also vanishes
due to the contraction with
a_{abc}
.

One-forms

The equation for transverse one-forms (
D^{a}Y_{a}=0
or, equivalently,
\check{D}^{a}Y_{a}=0
) reads

\Delta_{1}Y_{a}=\kappa^{2}_{1}Y_{a}.

(46)

Again using
Equation

40
we get, now with
C_{so(7)}(\bm{7})=3
and
C_{g_{2}}(\bm{7})=2
,

\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}Y_{a}\equiv a_{a}{}^{bc}\check{D}_{c}Y_{b}=\sqrt{5}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{1}+\frac{3}{5}\biggr)Y_{a}.

(47)

The standard procedure at this point is to square the operator, i.e., act with
\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
again, which gives

(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1})^{2}Y_{a}=-\check{\Box}Y_{a}+\check{D}^{b}\check{D}_{a}Y_{b}+c_{a}{}^{bcd}\check{D}_{b}\check{D}_{c}Y_{d}=\Bigl(C_{g}-C_{h}+\frac{24}{5}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}\Bigr)Y_{a},

(48)

where we have used that
Y_{a}
is transverse.
We have also used the Ricci identity on one-forms

[\check{D}_{a},\check{D}_{b}]Y_{c}=\Bigl(W_{abc}{}^{d}+\frac{6}{5}(P_{14})_{abc}{}^{d}\Bigr)Y_{d}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}a_{abd}\check{D}^{d}Y_{c},

(49)

and the following two direct implications of it:

[\check{D}^{b},\check{D}_{a}]Y_{b}=\frac{12}{5}Y_{a}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}Y_{a},

(50)

and

c_{a}{}^{bcd}\check{D}_{b}\check{D}_{c}Y_{d}=\frac{12}{5}Y_{a}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}Y_{a}.

(51)

It is interesting to note that
C_{h}
reappeared in the above calculation of
(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1})^{2}Y_{a}
but that the formula for the Weyl tensor
Equation

38
applied to one-forms gives rise to a contracted
Weyl tensor which vanishes and hence gives (also obtained in
Duff:1986hr
)

\biggl(\frac{12}{5}-C_{h}\biggr)Y_{a}=0.

(52)

Hence, the eigenvalue equation
Equation

48
becomes

C_{g}+\frac{12}{5}-\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{1}+\frac{3}{5}\biggr)=5\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{1}+\frac{3}{5}\biggr)^{2},

(53)

which is solved by
\kappa^{2}_{1}=\Delta^{(1)_{\pm}}_{1}
, where

\Delta^{(1)_{\pm}}_{1}=C_{g}+\frac{7}{10}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{49}{20}}.

(54)

Two-forms

We now turn to two-forms and the eigenvalue equation

\Delta_{2}Y_{ab}=\kappa_{2}^{2}Y_{ab}.

(55)

In the previous literature the additional technical issues that start to appear here were handled on a case-by-case basis, leading to poor understanding and long calculations.
In
Nilsson:2018lof
, for instance, equations like
Equation

40
were derived for the different types of fields separately.
Moreover, they still contained
C_{h}
and the Weyl tensor, presenting further technical difficulties, since the latter appears when acting with
\Delta_{p}
on a
p
-form, cf.
Equation

9
, and its expression in terms of Casimirs,
Equation

38
, was not known.
Using the novel version of the key equations
Equation

38
and
Equation

40
this computation will be much easier as we now show.

In the improved formalism of this paper

Equation

40
takes the following form when applied to transverse
two-forms
8
8

8

The reason for the square bracket on the index 2, here and below, is that the free indices are antisymmetrised.
The same differential operator but without antisymmetrisation or with symmetrised indices will be used below.

\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}\equiv a_{[a}{}^{cd}\check{D}_{|d}Y_{c|b]}=\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2}(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{2}+3P_{7})Y_{ab}.

(56)

Here we have used
C_{so(7)}(\mathbf{21})=5
,
C_{g_{2}}(\mathbf{7})=2
and
C_{g_{2}}(\mathbf{14})=4
. Note that the reason
P_{7}
appears in the last expression is that
the two-form splits under
Spin(7)\rightarrow G_{2}
into
\bf{14}
and
\bf{7}
which have different
C_{g_{2}}
-eigenvalues. Note also that the transversality constraint reads,
with
G_{2}
-covariant derivatives,

\check{D}^{b}Y_{ba}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{5}}a_{abc}Y^{bc}.

(57)

In order to proceed it is very convenient to define a second linear operator

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}\equiv a_{[a}{}^{cd}\check{D}_{b]}Y_{cd}\equiv-\check{D}_{[a}Y_{b]},

(58)

where we in the last equality have defined the vector
Y_{a}
associated to the two-form by

Y_{a}\equiv a_{a}{}^{bc}Y_{bc}.

(59)

Note that this vector field is not transverse a priori (as it is in the one-form case above), a fact that will become important below.

One can quite easily derive a relation between the two linear derivative operators defined above. It reads

c_{ab}{}^{cd}\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{cd}=\frac{3}{\sqrt{5}}P_{7}Y_{ab}+2\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}-2\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}.

(60)

By writing
c_{ab}{}^{cd}
in terms of
\delta_{ab}^{cd}
and
(P_{7})_{ab}{}^{cd}
, this can be rewritten as

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}=(2-3P_{7})\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}+\frac{3}{2\sqrt{5}}P_{7}Y_{ab}.

(61)

This equation implies that, if
Y_{ab}
has no
{\bf{7}}
-part, it will satisfy
\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}=0
since, in that case,
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}=0=P_{7}Y_{ab}
and

2-3P_{7}
is invertible when acting on two-forms.

Combining
Equations

56
and
61
leads to the following much simpler equation for the eigenvalues

\check{D}_{[a}Y_{b]}=-\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2}\left(2\bigl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{2}\bigr)-3\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{2}+\frac{4}{5}\biggr)P_{7}\right)Y_{ab}.

(62)

The reason this is simpler than the original eigenvalue equation is that it becomes an eigenvalue equation for one-forms when the
G_{2}
part
\mathbf{7}
of the
Spin(7)
-irrep
\mathbf{21}

is extracted by contracting with
a_{abc}
. Doing this we find (for
\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
see the one-form analysis above)

\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}Y_{a}=-\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2}\left(C_{g}-\kappa_{2}^{2}+\frac{12}{5}\right)Y_{a}.

(63)

To deal with the lack of transversality of the one-form
Y_{a}
we contract the last equation above by
\check{D}^{a}
and find that either
\check{D}^{a}Y_{a}=0

or (again denoting the eigenvalues by indexed operators)

\Delta_{2}^{(1)}=C_{g}+\frac{18}{5}.

(64)

In the former case,
\check{D}^{a}Y_{a}=0
, we repeat the one-form analysis starting from
Equation

63
and find that either
Y_{a}=0
or

\Delta_{2}^{(2)_{\pm}}=C_{g}+\frac{11}{5}\pm\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{49}{20}}.

(65)

When
Y_{a}=0
then also
\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}=0
, as remarked above, and we see from
Equation

56
that the last set of eigenvalues are given by

\Delta_{2}^{(3)}=C_{g}.

(66)

Lichnerowicz

We now turn to the case of symmetric transverse and traceless rank-2 tensors which we will denote as
X_{ab}
.
Thus
X_{ab}
satisfies

\Delta X_{ab}=\kappa^{2}_{L}X_{ab},\qquad X^{a}{}_{a}=0,\qquad D^{a}X_{ab}=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \check{D}^{a}X_{ab}=0.

(67)

Using
C_{so(7)}({\bf 27})=7
and
C_{g_{2}}({\bf 27})=\frac{14}{3}
, the master formula becomes

\check{\mathcal{D}}_{(2)}X_{ab}\equiv a_{(a}{}^{cd}\check{D}_{|d}X_{c|b)}=\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{L}+\frac{7}{5}\biggr)X_{ab},

(68)

where we have introduced the differential operator
\check{\mathcal{D}}_{(2)}
,
which clearly is the object we need to study and if possible relate its action on
X_{ab}
to
\kappa_{L}^{2}
and
X_{ab}
itself.

It turns out, however, that it is much more convenient to start by considering the operator

(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}X)_{ab}\equiv a_{a}{}^{cd}\check{D}_{d}X_{cb},

(69)

without any kind of symmetrisation. The reason for this is that
one can rather easily derive a nice equation for its square
(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}{}^{2}X)_{ab}
.

To proceed we note that
(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}X)_{ab}
is traceless and has vanishing
{\bf{7}}
-part and hence
contains only the irreducible parts
{\bf{14}}
and
{\bf{27}}
. The latter,
(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{(2)}X)_{ab}
, was defined above while the former will from now on be denoted

Y_{ab}\equiv(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}X)_{ab}=(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}X)_{ab}-kX_{ab},

(70)

where
k
is the coefficient on the right-hand side of the master equation,
Equation

68
.
It is easy to show that
\check{D}^{a}(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}X)_{ab}=0
, which by the above equation implies that
Y_{ab}
is transverse (
D^{a}Y_{ab}=0
) since
X_{ab}
is transverse and
Y_{ab}
has no
\mathbf{7}
-part.

It is now fairly easy to compute
(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}{}^{2}X)_{ab}
.
In fact, from
Equation

38
we find

2W_{a}{}^{c}{}_{b}{}^{d}X_{cd}=\biggl(\frac{28}{5}-C_{h}\biggr)X_{ab}

(71)

(found earlier in
Ekhammar:2021gsg
) which together with the Ricci identity for
\check{D}_{a}
makes it possible to show that
C_{h}
cancels and that

(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}{}^{2}X)_{ab}=-\check{\Box}X_{ab}+\check{D}^{c}\check{D}_{a}X_{cb}+c_{a}{}^{cde}\check{D}_{c}\check{D}_{d}X_{eb}=C_{g}X_{ab}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}X)_{ab}.

(72)

Finally, by combining this equation with the one defining
Y_{ab}
above we find

\biggl(k^{2}+\frac{k}{\sqrt{5}}-C_{g}\biggr)X_{ab}=-\biggl(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}+k+\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\biggr)Y_{ab}.

(73)

To solve this equation we consider first its antisymmetric part which reads

\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}=-\biggl(k+\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\biggr)Y_{ab}=-\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{L}+\frac{9}{5}\biggr)Y_{ab}.

(74)

However,
\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}=0
since
Y_{ab}
has only a
\mathbf{14}
-part, as already noted above when solving for the two-form eigenvalues. Thus either
Y_{ab}=0
or
\kappa_{L}^{2}=\Delta_{L}^{(1)}=C_{g}+\frac{9}{5}
.

When
Y_{ab}=0
, we have, by
Equation

73
,

\biggl(k^{2}+\frac{k}{\sqrt{5}}-C_{g}\biggr)X_{ab}=0.

(75)

Inserting the expression for
k
above and solving this equation give the remaining two sets of eigenvalues,
\Delta^{(2)_{\pm}}_{L}
.

To summarise, we have found that the eigenvalues of the Lichnerowicz operator are just the following three sets (reported without derivation already in
Duff:1986hr
)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(1)}_{L}=C_{g}+\frac{9}{5},

(76)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(2)_{\pm}}_{L}=C_{g}+\frac{8}{5}\pm\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{1}{20}}.

(77)

Three-forms

Following the by now standard procedure, we start by presenting the master formula applied to three-forms, in the irrep
{\bf{35}}
of
so(7)
. Thus,
since its
G_{2}
-content is given by
{\bf 35}\rightarrow{\bf 1}\oplus{\bf 7}\oplus{\bf 27}
we need
C_{so(7)}({\bf 27})=7
,
C_{g_{2}}({\bf 7})=2
and
C_{g_{2}}({\bf 27})=\frac{14}{3}
. Using these facts
and the eigenvalue equation for transverse three-forms

\Delta Y_{abc}=\kappa^{2}_{3}Y_{abc},\qquad\quad D^{a}Y_{abc}=0,

(78)

the master formula becomes

3a_{[a}{}^{de}\check{D}_{|e}Y_{d|bc]}=\sqrt{5}\left(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{3}+\frac{36}{5}-3P_{7}-7P_{27}\right)Y_{abc}.

(79)

With some care when imposing transversality we can make use of previous results, in particular the ones related to Lichnerowicz discussed just above.

To split the three-form into its
G_{2}
parts according to
{\bf 35}\rightarrow{\bf 1}\oplus{\bf 7}\oplus{\bf 27}
we use
the projection operators in
Appendix

B
.
This leads to

Y_{abc}=\frac{1}{42}a_{abc}Y+\frac{1}{24}c_{abc}{}^{d}Y_{d}+\frac{3}{4}a_{[ab}{}^{d}X_{c]d},

(80)

where

Y\equiv a^{abc}Y_{abc},\quad Y_{a}\equiv-c_{a}{}^{bcd}Y_{bcd},\,\,\,X_{ab}\equiv a_{(a}{}^{cd}Y_{b)cd}-\frac{1}{7}\delta_{ab}Y.

(81)

The transversality condition
D^{a}Y_{abc}=0
in terms of
\check{D}_{a}
translates into

\check{D}^{c}Y_{cab}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}a_{[a}{}^{cd}Y_{b]cd}.

(82)

The key equation
Equation

79
above splits as the three-form itself into three pieces. Considering first the parts
{\bf 1}
and
{\bf 7}
we get

\displaystyle 3\check{D}^{a}Y_{a}={\sqrt{5}}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{3}+\frac{36}{5}\biggr)Y,

(83)

\displaystyle\frac{4}{7}\check{D}_{a}Y-\frac{1}{6}a_{a}{}^{bc}\check{D}_{b}Y_{c}-3\check{D}^{b}X_{ba}=-\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{3}+\frac{21}{5}\biggr)Y_{a}.

(84)

To extract the eigenvalues connected to these two equations we note that the term
\check{D}^{b}X_{ba}
in the latter one is also occurring in the
{\bf 7}
part of the transversality condition
and can thus be replaced by terms involving only
Y
and
Y_{a}
. Doing this,
Equation

84
gives rise to an equation which we contract by
\check{D}^{a}
and use

Equation

83
to eliminate
\check{D}^{a}Y_{a}
. The final result is

\frac{9}{5}C_{g}Y=\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{3}+\frac{9}{5}\biggr)\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{3}+\frac{36}{5}\biggr)Y,

(85)

which, if
Y\neq 0
gives the eigenvalues
\kappa^{2}_{3}=\Delta_{3}^{(3)_{\pm}}
,

\Delta_{3}^{(3)_{\pm}}=C_{g}+\frac{9}{2}\pm\frac{3}{\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{81}{20}}.

(86)

If instead
Y=0
we see from the above equations that
\check{D}^{a}Y_{a}=0
, which implies
{D}^{a}Y_{a}=0
, and that
Y_{a}
satisfies an
equation which, apart from having different constants, is identical to the one-form one above. Repeating the steps
taken there we find the following result for the eigenvalues associated with
Y_{a}
in the present case:

\Delta_{3}^{(2)_{\pm}}=C_{g}+\frac{5}{2}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{49}{20}}.

(87)

Finally, if instead of getting the previous set of eigenvalues also
Y_{a}=0
then only
X_{ab}
is left in
Y_{abc}
. Now the transversality condition gives

\check{D}^{b}X_{ba}=0,\qquad\quad P_{14}(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}X)_{ab}=0,

(88)

and the master equation
Equation

79
reduces to

(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}X)_{ab}=-{\sqrt{5}}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa^{2}_{3}+\frac{1}{5}\biggr)X_{ab}.

(89)

The situation has now become similar to the one for the Lichnerowicz operator but with the additional information that what we there called
Y_{ab}=(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}X)_{ab}
vanishes since
P_{14}(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}X)_{ab}=0
and the
\bm{7}
-part of
Y_{ab}
always vanish for transverse
X_{ab}
, as noted above.
Hence, we only get the case analogous to
Equation

75
, not
Equation

74
.
Changing the constants of the Lichnerowicz analysis appropriately, this gives the two eigenvalues in
Equation

90
.

Thus we have found the following three branches of eigenvalues for the three-forms:

\displaystyle\Delta_{3}^{(1)_{\pm}}=C_{g}+\frac{1}{10}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{1}{20}},

(90)

\displaystyle\Delta_{3}^{(2)_{\pm}}=C_{g}+\frac{5}{2}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{49}{20}},

(91)

\displaystyle\Delta_{3}^{(3)_{\pm}}=C_{g}+\frac{9}{2}\pm\frac{3}{\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{81}{20}}.

(92)

When we summarise these results at the end of this section we will instead give the eigenvalues of the linear operator
Q
related to
\Delta_{3}
by

\Delta_{3}=Q^{2}
. This will force us to choose one of two possible signs which, however, can be done using supersymmetry as will be clear from
the analysis in
Section

5
.

Spin 1/2

When we now turn to operator equations on the squashed seven-sphere for modes in spinorial tangent space representations
we follow the strategy in
Ekhammar:2021gsg
of squaring the corresponding Dirac equation. For the spin-1/2 case we thus consider

\Delta\psi=\kappa_{1/2}^{2}\psi,

(93)

where the universal Laplacian in
Equation

9
is related to the square of the Dirac operator by

\Delta\psi=(i\not{D})^{2}\psi-\frac{189}{80}\psi.

(94)

As in
Ekhammar:2021gsg
we will make heavy use of the
G_{2}
-invariant Killing spinor
\eta
which satisfies

\check{D}_{a}\eta=0.

(95)

Using
\eta
, we can define the projection operators needed to split the
Spin(7)
spinor irrep
{\bf 8}
into
G_{2}
parts according to

{\bf 8}\rightarrow{\bf 1}\oplus{\bf 7}
:

P_{1}\equiv\eta\bar{\eta},\qquad\quad P_{7}\equiv\Gamma^{a}\eta\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{a}.

(96)

These projection operators satisfy
P_{1}+P_{7}=1
(which is just a Fierz identity) which leads to the following splitting of the spinor:

\psi=Y\eta+iY_{a}\Gamma^{a}\eta.

(97)

where we have defined two real tensor fields

Y\equiv\bar{\eta}\psi,\qquad\quad Y_{a}\equiv-i\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{a}\psi.

(98)

Using the fact that
C_{so(7)}({\bf 8})=21/8
we find that the master formula reads

a_{abc}\Gamma^{ab}\check{D}^{c}\psi=4\sqrt{5}\left(C_{g}-\kappa_{1/2}^{2}+\frac{63}{20}-3P_{7}\right)\psi.

(99)

This equation can be projected onto its scalar and vector parts leading to the following two equations

\displaystyle 3\check{D}^{a}Y_{a}=2\sqrt{5}\left(C_{g}-\kappa_{1/2}^{2}+\frac{63}{20}\right)Y,

(100)

\displaystyle 3\check{D}_{a}Y+a_{a}{}^{bc}\check{D}_{c}Y_{b}=-2\sqrt{5}\left(C_{g}-\kappa_{1/2}^{2}+\frac{3}{20}\right)Y_{a}.

(101)

Here, we see immediately that if
Y=0
then
Y_{a}
is a transverse one-form satisfying an equation of the form
\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}Y_{a}\propto Y_{a}
.
Thus, we first want to use the two equations above to derive the eigenvalues in the
Y\neq 0
case to then be able to reuse the analysis of the one-forms.

This is done as follows. We contract the latter of the two equations above by
\check{D}^{a}
and use the first equation to eliminate the
\check{D}^{a}Y_{a}
term.
This gives

\frac{9}{20}C_{g}Y=\left(C_{g}-\kappa_{1/2}^{2}+\frac{9}{20}\right)\left(C_{g}+\kappa_{1/2}^{2}+\frac{63}{20}\right)Y.

(102)

Thus either
Y=0
or the eigenvalue
\kappa_{1/2}^{2}
is given by the solution to this equation, which reads

\Delta_{1/2}^{(1)}=C_{g}+\frac{9}{2}\pm\frac{3}{2\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{81}{20}}.

(103)

On the other hand, when
Y=0
the above equations
Equations

100
and
101
reduce to the equations for a transverse one-form analysed previously.
Reusing that computation we find

\Delta_{1/2}^{(2)}=C_{g}+\frac{1}{10}\pm\frac{1}{2\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{49}{20}}.

(104)

The final step is to solve for the eigenvalues of the Dirac operator
i\not{D}_{1/2}
from
Equation

94
.
The result is given as
i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(1)_{\pm}}
and
i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)_{\pm}}
in
Section

3.3
.

3.2
The novel case of spin 3/2

The last operator equation to discuss is the spin-3/2 one.
This is also the one that has not been solved previously in the literature (see however
Karlsson:2021oxd
)
but which can now be dealt with using the more efficient methods presented in the previous section.
As we will see below the analysis of the spin-3/2 eigenvalue equation will contain most of the intricate features encountered,
and resolved, in connection with the cases discussed above.

The starting point here is the same as for the other cases above:
Write out the master formula, that is
Equation

40
, using
C_{so(7)}({\bf{48}})=\frac{49}{8}
,
C_{g_{2}}({\bf{7}})=2
,
C_{g_{2}}({\bf{14}})=4
and
C_{g_{2}}({\bf{27}})=\frac{14}{3}
,

a_{a}{}^{bc}\check{D}_{c}\psi_{b}+\frac{1}{4}a_{de}{}^{c}\Gamma^{de}\check{D}_{c}\psi_{a}=\sqrt{5}\left(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{147}{20}-3P_{7}-6P_{14}-7P_{27}\right)\psi_{a},

(105)

which is satisfied for transverse and gamma-traceless vector-spinors
\psi_{a}
, i.e.,
D^{a}\psi_{a}=0
and
\Gamma^{a}\psi_{a}=0
. Here
\kappa_{3/2}^{2}
is the eigenvalue of the universal Laplacian
which is related the square of the spin-3/2 operator
i\not{D}_{3/2}
, i.e., we have,

\Delta\psi_{a}=\kappa_{3/2}^{2}\psi_{a},\qquad\quad\Delta\psi_{a}=({i\not{D}})^{2}\psi_{a}+\frac{27}{80}\psi_{a}.

(106)

When giving the result of this subsection in the summary at the end of this section it is the eigenvalues of
{i\not{D}}_{3/2}
that are quoted.
This step requires taking the square root of the result for
({i\not{D}}_{3/2})^{2}
giving a sign ambiguity that is resolved by requiring that
the eigenvalues must be consistent with supersymmetry in the case of the left-squashed
S^{7}
compactification. This is analysed in detail in
Section

5
.

Irreducible
G_{2}
-components and transversality

The strategy we will adopt to analyse the above spin-3/2 equation will rely on the fact that the
G_{2}
-content in
{\bf 48}
is
{\bf 7}\oplus{\bf 14}\oplus{\bf 27}
which
corresponds precisely to a traceless two-tensor without symmetrisations: The antisymmetric part, denoted
Y_{ab}
, corresponds to
{\bf 7}\oplus{\bf 14}
while the traceless symmetric one,

X_{ab}
, corresponds to
{\bf 27}
. This makes it possible to translate
the present spin-3/2 problem into problems involving two-tensors and hence to use knowledge obtained from the previous subsection.
As in the spin-1/2 case above, we can use the Killing spinor
\eta
(and the Fierz identity
\Gamma_{a}\eta\bar{\eta}\Gamma^{a}=1-\eta\bar{\eta}
) to define the
split
Bais:1983wc
;
Duff:1984sv
;
Duff:1986hr

\psi_{a}\equiv Y_{a}\eta+i\Gamma^{b}Z_{ba}\eta,

(107)

where the two (real) tensors are given by

Y_{a}\equiv\bar{\eta}\psi_{a},\qquad\quad Z_{ab}\equiv-i\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{a}\psi_{b}.

(108)

Note that the two-tensor appearing here has no specified symmetry on the indices. However, from the gamma-tracelessness of the spin-3/2 field,
\Gamma^{a}\psi_{a}=0
, we see that
Z_{a}{}^{a}=0
.
Thus, the only remaining issue is whether or not there is an independent
{\bf 7}
piece in
Z_{ab}
. To clarify this point we compute

a_{a}{}^{bc}Z_{bc}=\bar{\eta}\Gamma^{abc}\eta\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{b}\psi_{c}=-\bar{\eta}\Gamma^{ac}\psi_{c}=Y^{a},

(109)

where we have used the above Fierz identity for
\eta
together with
\Gamma^{a}\psi_{a}=0
and the relation
a_{abc}=i\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{abc}\eta
(see
Appendix

A
).
Thus it is convenient to define the
G_{2}
irreducible parts of
Z_{ab}
as follows (for the projection operators, see
Appendix

B
)

X_{ab}\equiv Z_{(ab)},\qquad Y_{ab}\equiv Z_{[ab]},\qquad Y^{7}_{ab}=P_{7}Y_{ab},\qquad Y^{14}_{ab}=P_{14}Y_{ab},

(110)

where
Y^{7}_{ab}=\frac{1}{6}a_{ab}{}^{c}Y_{c}
.
Thus we see that the content of independent tensors in the spin-3/2 field
\psi_{a}
is exactly one vector (
\bm{7}
), one
\bm{14}
-part of an antisymmetric two-tensor and one traceless symmetric two-tensor (
\bm{27}
).

Before attempting to find the spin-3/2 eigenvalues
\kappa_{3/2}^{2}
defined in
Equation

105
, we also need to analyse the transversality condition of
\psi_{a}
.
In terms of
\check{D}_{a}
, the transversality condition reads

\check{D}^{a}\psi_{a}=-\frac{1}{8\sqrt{5}}a^{abc}\Gamma_{ab}\psi_{c}.

(111)

By contracting with
\bar{\eta}
and
\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{a}
, we can extract its
\bm{1}
and
\bm{7}
parts, respectively,

\check{D}^{a}Y_{a}=0,\qquad\quad\check{D}^{b}Z_{ab}=\check{D}^{b}X_{ba}-\check{D}^{b}Y_{ba}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}Y_{a},

(112)

where we have used

i\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{a}\Gamma^{cd}\psi_{b}=a_{a}{}^{cd}Y_{b}+c_{a}{}^{cde}Z_{eb}-\delta_{a}^{c}Z^{d}{}_{b}+\delta_{a}^{d}Z^{c}{}_{b},

(113)

which follows from
Equation

107
. These equations will be used below.

Deriving the eigenvalues

To find the eigenvalues associated to the various tensors obtained in the above decomposition of
\psi_{a}
,
we start by converting
Equation

105
into an equation for
Z_{ab}
.
9
9

9

The eigenmodes and eigenvalues are not strictly associated to the
G_{2}
-irreducible pieces since
\Delta
is block-triangular rather than block-diagonal over these, as we will see below.

Contracting it with
\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{a}
and
using
Equation

113
, we find

-\frac{3}{2\sqrt{5}}\check{D}_{a}Y_{b}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}a_{b}{}^{cd}\check{D}_{d}Z_{ac}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{5}}a_{a}{}^{cd}\check{D}_{d}Z_{cb}=\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{7}{20}+P_{14}+4P_{7}\biggr)Z_{ab}.

(114)

Here we note that the first term contains
Y_{a}
.

In order to get further equations involving
Y_{a}
we need to discuss the various divergences (see, e.g.,
Equation

112
). Note first that

\check{D}^{b}Y^{7}_{ba}=\frac{1}{6}a_{a}{}^{cb}\check{D}_{b}Y_{c}=\frac{1}{6}\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}Y_{a},

(115)

and that
Equation

112
relates the divergences of
X_{ab}
and
Y_{ab}
to
Y_{a}
.
To get other equations relating the divergences of the two-index tensors, we can contract
Equation

114
with
\check{D}^{a}
and
\check{D}^{b}
.
This will lead to a situation resembling the analysis of one-forms carried out in the previous subsection. Note that
Y_{a}
satisfies the transversality
condition
Equation

112
which implies
D^{a}Y_{a}=0
.

By contracting
Equation

114
with
\check{D}^{b}
we get a second relation between the divergences of
Y^{7}_{ab}
,
Y^{14}_{ab}
and
X_{ab}
.
Combining this with the transversality condition for
\psi_{a}
,
Equation

112
, we can solve for two of them as

\displaystyle\check{D}^{b}Y^{14}_{ba}

\displaystyle=-3\check{D}^{b}Y^{7}_{ba}+\frac{\sqrt{5}}{3}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{81}{20}\biggr)Y_{a},

(116)

\displaystyle\check{D}^{b}X_{ba}

\displaystyle=-2\check{D}^{b}Y^{7}_{ba}+\frac{\sqrt{5}}{3}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{93}{20}\biggr)Y_{a}.

(117)

Next, we contract
Equation

114
with
\check{D}^{a}
.
The calculation is a bit involved, more details can be found in
Karlsson:2021oxd
, but again gives an equation relating the divergences of the two-index tensors.
Using
Equations

116
and
117
to eliminate
\check{D}^{b}Y^{14}_{ba}
and
\check{D}^{b}X^{14}_{ba}
and
Equation

115
to convert
\check{D}^{b}Y^{7}_{ba}
to
\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}Y_{a}
, the result can be written as

\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{109}{40}\biggr)\check{\mathcal{D}}_{1}Y_{a}=\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2}\biggl[\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{57}{20}\biggr)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\biggl(C_{g}+\frac{12}{5}\biggr)\biggr]Y_{a}.

(118)

To find the eigenvalues from this equation we recall the analysis of the one-form above, in particular
Equations

47
and
48
.
It is then easy to see that in the present case it leads to a fourth-order equation in
\kappa_{3/2}^{2}
with solutions

\Delta_{3/2}^{(3)}=C_{g}+\frac{14}{5}\pm\frac{1}{2\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{49}{20}},\qquad\Delta_{3/2}^{(4)}=C_{g}+\frac{31}{10}\pm\frac{5}{2\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{49}{20}},

(119)

if
Y_{a}\neq 0
.

If, on the other hand,
Y_{a}=0
, then both
X_{ab}
and
Y^{14}_{ab}=Y_{ab}
are transverse by
Equations

116
and
117
.
Now, the situation is a bit similar to part of the three-form analysis, where we also had
\bm{14}
and
\bm{27}
pieces.
To proceed, we pick out the symmetric and antisymmetric pieces of
Equation

114
, giving

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{D}}_{(2)}X_{ab}-3(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{(2)}Y)_{ab}=2\sqrt{5}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{7}{20}\biggr)X_{ab},

(120)

\displaystyle-3(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}X)_{ab}+\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}=2\sqrt{5}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{27}{20}\biggr)Y_{ab}.

(121)

Recall, again, from the two-form analysis the very useful fact that a transverse two-form without
\bm{7}
-part satisfies
\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}Y_{ab}=0
.
Thus, we can form a linear combination of
Equations

120
and
121
only containing the operator
\mathcal{D}_{2}
as

(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}X)_{ab}-3(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}Y)_{ab}=2\sqrt{5}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{7}{20}\biggr)X_{ab}-\frac{2\sqrt{5}}{3}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{27}{20}\biggr)Y_{ab}.

(122)

To see why this is useful, we recall another piece of analysis from the previous subsection, namely for the Lichnerowicz operator, and in particular the equation
Equation

72

(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}{}^{2}X)_{ab}=C_{g}X_{ab}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}X)_{ab},

(123)

for transverse traceless symmetric rank-2 tensors
X_{ab}
.
A short calculation shows that the same relation also holds for transverse two-forms.
If we act with
\check{\mathcal{D}}_{2}
on
Equation

122
, use this relation and then pick out the antisymmetric part, we get an equation relating
Y_{ab}
to
(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}X)_{ab}
, namely

C_{g}Y_{ab}=-\frac{2\sqrt{5}}{3}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{9}{20}\biggr)(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}X)_{ab}.

(124)

Importantly, since we used
Equation

123
, this equation is linearly independent from
Equation

121
and they can be combined into

C_{g}Y_{ab}=\frac{20}{9}\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{9}{20}\biggr)\biggl(C_{g}-\kappa_{3/2}^{2}+\frac{27}{20}\biggr)Y_{ab}.

(125)

Thus, either
Y_{ab}=0
or

\Delta_{3/2}^{(2)}=C_{g}+\frac{9}{10}\pm\frac{3}{2\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{9}{20}}.

(126)

In the last case,
Y_{ab}=0
, when only the
\bm{27}
-part is non-vanishing
Equations

120
and
121
gives us the Lichnerowicz situation reviewed above but with the additional information that
(\check{\mathcal{D}}_{[2]}X)_{ab}=0
.
As it turns out, this is exactly the situation from the three-form analysis with only a non-vanishing
\bm{27}
-part, also reviewed in the previous subsection.
The implication from that analysis is then, again, that we only get two eigenvalues, given in
Equation

130
below.

In summary, we have found that all eigenvalues of
\Delta_{3/2}
fall into one of the following sets:

\displaystyle\Delta_{3/2}^{(3)}=C_{g}+\frac{14}{5}\pm\frac{1}{2\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{49}{20}},

(127)

\displaystyle\Delta_{3/2}^{(4)}=C_{g}+\frac{31}{10}\pm\frac{5}{2\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{49}{20}},

(128)

\displaystyle\Delta_{3/2}^{(2)}=C_{g}+\frac{9}{10}\pm\frac{3}{2\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{9}{20}},

(129)

\displaystyle\Delta_{3/2}^{(1)}=C_{g}+\frac{2}{5}\pm\frac{1}{2\sqrt{5}}\sqrt{C_{g}+\frac{1}{20}}.

(130)

Using
Equation

106
, and supersymmetry to fix the sign ambiguity, it is possible to determine the eigenvalues of
{i\not{D}}_{3/2}
, which are denoted
{i\not{D}}_{3/2}^{(i)_{\pm}}
,
i=3,4,2,1
, in the summary below.

3.3
Summary: The entire squashed
S^{7}
operator eigenvalue spectrum

In
Section

5
we will use the results of this section, and the next, to specify the content of irreps

D(E_{0},s)
of all supermultiplets in the left-squashed vacuum.
To facilitate that discussion we here summarise the results on the left-squashed seven-sphere operator spectra we have obtained in this section:

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,20C_{g},

(131)

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}^{(1)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,\Bigl(20C_{g}+14\pm 2\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\Bigr)=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,\Bigl(\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\pm 1\Bigr)^{2}-4m^{2},

(132)

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,(20C_{g}+72),

(133)

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}^{(2)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,\Bigl(20C_{g}+44\pm 4\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\Bigr)=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,\bigl(\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\pm 2\bigr)^{2}-m^{2},

(134)

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,20C_{g},

(135)

\displaystyle\Delta_{L}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,(20C_{g}+36),

(136)

\displaystyle\Delta_{L}^{(2)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,\Bigl(20C_{g}+32\pm 4\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\Bigr)=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,\Bigl(\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\pm 2\Bigr)^{2}+3m^{2},

(137)

\displaystyle Q^{(1)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{3}\Bigl(-1\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\Bigr),

(138)

\displaystyle Q^{(2)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{3}\Bigl(1\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\Bigr),

(139)

\displaystyle Q^{(3)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{3}\Bigl(3\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}\Bigr),

(140)

\displaystyle i{\not{D}}_{1/2}^{(1)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{3}\biggl(\frac{3}{2}\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}\biggr),

(141)

\displaystyle i{\not{D}}_{1/2}^{(2)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{3}\biggl(-\frac{1}{2}\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\biggr),

(142)

\displaystyle i{\not{D}}_{3/2}^{(1)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{3}\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\biggr),

(143)

\displaystyle i{\not{D}}_{3/2}^{(2)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{3}\biggl(-\frac{3}{2}\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+9}}\biggr),

(144)

\displaystyle i{\not{D}}_{3/2}^{(3)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{3}\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\biggr),

(145)

\displaystyle i{\not{D}}_{3/2}^{(4)_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{3}\biggl(\frac{5}{2}\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\biggr),

(146)

Note that all linear operators have a sign ambiguity from the calculations above since those analyses used quadratic operators and gave the squares of the eigenvalues of the linear operators. However, as we will see in
Section

5
using supersymmetry, and for spinors already in
Section

4
, these
signs can be determined uniquely and these results has been used when quoting the eigenvalues above.
10
10

10

There is one exception were this sign ambiguity is not resolved uniquely, namely the
(p,q;r)=(0,1;0)
isometry irrep in which
Q^{(1)_{-}}=-10m/3
and
-Q^{(1)_{+}}=-8m/3
give rise to the same
M^{2}
using the formula in
Table

3
. We conjecture that the former is the correct eigenvalue based on the methods of the next section but either way this has no bearing on the
AdS_{4}
analysis of
Section

5
.

In
Section

5
we will fit all these eigenvalues into the supermultiplets that were established to be present in the left-squashed seven-sphere spectrum in

Nilsson:2018lof
. These supermultiplets are one spin
2^{+}
, six spin
3/2
, six spin
1^{+}
, eight spin
1^{-}
and 14 Wess–Zumino supermultiplets.
This procedure shows that there are degenerate
E_{0}
values of supermultiplets as well as operator eigenvalues that are associated with more than one set of modes.
This curious fact requires a deeper analysis of these modes. One such
analysis is carried out for two-forms in the next section proving explicitly that the degeneracy deduced from the supersymmetry analysis is realised in this case.

4
Associating isometry irreps with eigenvalues through eigenmodes

The purpose of this section is to provide a better understanding of the connection between isometry irreps and eigenvalues, i.e., which eigenvalue formulas from the previous section apply to which isometry irreps, through individual eigenmodes in the various operator spectra.
The eigenvalue results in the previous section and in previous works like

Ekhammar:2021gsg
;
Karlsson:2021oxd
were obtained using methods that did not rely on writing down explicit modes
and hence could not give a precise relation to the spectra of isometry irreps, which was derived in full detail in
Nilsson:2018lof
.
Exceptions to this where explicit modes have been constructed are spinors
Nilsson:1983ru
, one-forms
Yamagishi:1983ri

and the entire singlet sector
Nilsson:2023ctq
.

Lacking this connection between eigenvalues and isometry irreps has led to a potential problem, namely that
there seems to be fewer distinguished eigenvalues than isometry irreps. For instance, for two-forms, which is the first instant where this phenomenon arises,
we found in the previous section four different eigenvalues that should be related to five different modes all having the same isometry irrep, namely the five

r=p
cross diagrams for two-forms in the appendix of
Nilsson:2018lof
. Similar properties can be identified for the Lichnerowicz operator (three eigenvalues and six modes), three-forms (six eigenvalues and eight modes)
and vector-spinors (eight eigenvalues and twelve modes).
However, the eigenvalues found in the previous section are consistent with supersymmetry for all modes in the left-squashed vacuum, as will be clear from the next section, indicating that there might not be a problem after all.

To get a better understanding of this issue we will construct all eight sets of two-form modes and compute their individual eigenvalues in this section.
The results obtained provide strong support for the existence of these degeneracies which are made explicit in the next section.
We start the discussion in this section by reviewing the one-form and spinor cases which will also define our notation and explain our approach,
which is in the spirit of the spinor analysis in
Nilsson:1983ru
.

The modes will, in all cases, be constructed by letting differential operators
\mathcal{Y}
(of orders zero
11
11

11

The zeroth order differential operators are just constants, like
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}_{a}=s_{a}{}^{i}
in
Equation

148
.
, one and two) act on a scalar mode functions
\phi
.
From
Nilsson:2018lof
, we know that the isometry irreps in the scalar Fourier expansion are
(p,q;p)
with
p\geq 0
and
q\geq 0
(all of multiplicity one) which we illustrate diagrammatically in a
cross diagram in
Figure

1
. As is well-known, the zero-form eigenvalues are
\Delta_{0}^{(1)}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,20C_{g}
(see
Section

3
).
In what follows, it is understood that these cross diagrams should be infinitely extended in the two positive directions and that
they are always made large enough to account for all special cases that arise for small
p
or
q
.

\Delta_{0}^{(1)}

1
2
3
4
1
2
3
4
0
p
0
q
r=p
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times
\times

\times

\times

Figure 1:
Scalar cross diagram. Each cross corresponds to an isometry irrep
(p,q;r)
, for
p\geq 0
,
q\geq 0
, of eigenmodes
\phi
of
\Delta_{0}
with eigenvalues

\Delta_{0}^{(1)}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,20C_{g}
, as given in
Equation
