. Note that the lines without a number in the leftmost column are continuation lines.

6
Conclusions and comments

In this paper, we have reported on technical progress on the computation of the eigenvalue spectra of operators
on the squashed seven-sphere relevant in the compactification of eleven-dimensional supergravity.
The full isometry irrep spectrum was derived in
Nilsson:2018lof
, including the structure of the
AdS_{4}
supermultiplet spectrum for the left-squashed vacuum.
With the results of
Karlsson:2021oxd
and this paper on the solution of the spin-3/2 equation,
these supermultiplets can be associated with values of
E_{0}
, pinpointing the
Spin(2,3)
-irrep
D(E_{0},s)
, thus providing a full understanding of the left-squashed spectrum.
26
26

26

Note that, as explained in
Section

5.1
, we use results of
Duboeuf:2022mam
to resolve some ambiguities that would otherwise appear for the supermultiplets denoted WZ3–6 for small
p
or
q
.

This is the case also after skew-whiffing to the right-squashed non-supersymmetric vacuum,
whose spectrum becomes fully determined up to a number of ambiguities connected to the possibility to choose either Dirichlet or Neumann boundary conditions
for a number of fields. Some ambiguities of this kind exist also in the supersymmetric left-squashed case.
These results are summarised in
Tables

4
–
13
of
Section

5
.

This progress was made possible by deriving more powerful versions of some key formulas used in solving operator equations on coset manifolds, see
Karlsson:2021oxd
and
Section

2
,
here the squashed
S^{7}
represented by the coset
Sp_{2}\times Sp_{1}^{C}/Sp_{1}\times Sp_{1}
. Using these refined formulas, in particular
Equation

38
and
Equation

40
,
we finally managed to solve
also the spin-3/2 equation. These results are summarised in
Section

3.3
but they were reported on already in
Karlsson:2021oxd
where some additional details can
be found compared to the presentation
in this paper.

The structure of the results is to a large extent as expected apart from some surprising features: We found degeneracies in the spectrum of two-forms, in the sense that
different mode functions have identical operator eigenvalues.
We demonstrated this explicitly by constructing all eigenmodes of the operator
\Delta_{2}
,
providing a direct means of calculating the eigenvalues.
These novel results are presented in
Section

4
. This kind of degeneracy appears in (almost) all supermultiplets that involve fields associated to the eigenmodes of the
Lichnerowicz operator on the squashed seven-sphere, as was shown in
Section

5
.

We also found that supersymmetry alone is not enough to determine the boundary conditions for a number of supermultiplets.
This issue was discussed in
Section

5
where the supermultiplet structure was presented in full detail.

The implications for the skew-whiffed non-supersymmetric right-squashed seven-sphere compactification are discussed at the end of the previous section.
Here, the boundary condition ambiguities appear for many fields which implies that one can find a large number of scalar singlet marginal operators
in the boundary
CFT_{3}
. This is of interest since
the right-squashed case is BF-stable and has, in fact, not yet been shown to be unstable, although this is predicted by the swampland

AdS
swampland conjecture
Ooguri:2016pdq
;
Freivogel:2016qwc
. However, in order to determine the fate of the right-squashed vacuum one must analyse the corresponding
\beta
functions
to determine whether a marginal operator leads to an instability or not. We leave this for a future study.
Interestingly enough, there are multiple sets of boundary conditions for which no scalar singlet marginal operators exists.
This phenomenon was recently observed in
Nilsson:2023ctq
to
be a property of the singlet sector of the spectrum.
27
27

27

Generally, we believe that Dirichlet boundary conditions can always be chosen in non-supersymmetric vacua such that the only possible scalar singlet marginal operators are single-trace operators or multi-trace operators dual to composites containing a singleton or fields with
E_{0}=3/2
.

Another property of the space of singlet scalar marginal operators found in
Section

5.2.3
is that there are none that are either single- or double-trace.
The implications of this fact might be worth investigating.

The squashed spectra clearly also imply that the boundary theories contain a large number of relevant operators which can generate RG-flows to other CFTs.
For instance, a double-trace operator constructed as the square of a single-trace operator dual to a scalar field with Neumann boundary conditions generates a flow that leads, in the infrared, to the dual scalar field having Dirichlet boundary conditions
Witten:2001ua
.
It would be interesting to investigate whether there are RG-flows connecting the boundary conditions preserving supersymmetry in the left-squashed vacuum.
Another interesting potential RG-flow is one that corresponds to a domain-wall interpolating between either of the squashed vacua and the round
S^{7}
vacuum in the bulk, as discussed, but not found explicitly, in
Ahn:1999dq
;
Campos:2000yu
(see also, e.g.,
Witten:2001ua
;
Papadimitriou:2007sj
;
Giombi:2017mxl
) or one that connects the left- and right-squashed vacua.

The operator corresponding to the field associated with the squashing mode (the
\Delta_{L}
-row in
Table

7
) is relevant in the squashed vacuum and irrelevant in the round vacuum
Nilsson:2023ctq
.
This indicates that an RG-flow connecting the two would have to start from the squashed vacuum in the UV and flow to the round one in the IR.
Another argument for this is provided by the F-theorem
Jafferis:2010un
;
Jafferis:2011zi
;
Closset:2012vg
, which states that the
S^{3}
free energy decreases along RG-trajectories.
Holographically, the free energy is given by
28
28

28

It should be understood that this, and what follows, is only valid to leading order in
N
or
L^{2}/G_{N}
.

\mathcal{F}_{\!S^{3}}=I_{EAdS}=\frac{\pi L^{2}}{2G_{N}},

(237)

where
I_{EAdS}
is the regularised on-shell action of Euclidean
AdS
,
L^{2}
the
AdS
curvature radius and
G_{N}
the four-dimensional Newton’s constant.
By dimensional reduction, we have
G_{N}\propto\ell_{\mathrm{Pl}}{}^{9}/\operatorname{Vol}(M_{7})
where
\ell_{\mathrm{Pl}}
is the eleven-dimensional Planck length.
The flux quantisation condition gives the number of M2-branes as
N\propto\operatorname{Vol}(M_{7})/(\ell_{\mathrm{Pl}}{}^{6}L)
.
Putting this together to eliminate
\ell_{\mathrm{Pl}}
, we get
\mathcal{F}_{\!S^{3}}\propto N^{3/2}L^{7/2}/\sqrt{\operatorname{Vol}(M_{7})}
.
Lastly, computing the volume of the squashed
S^{7}
and fixing the proportionality constant through the well-known round
S^{7}
answer or by properly keeping track of all numerical factors in the quantisation condition and the dimensional reduction (see, e.g.,
Jafferis:2011zi
), we arrive at (c.f.
Ahn:1999dq
;
Page:1983mke
;
Campos:2000yu
)

\mathcal{F}_{\!S^{3}}^{{\mkern 2.0mu}(\mathrm{round})}=\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{3}N^{3/2},\qquad\quad\mathcal{F}_{\!S^{3}}^{{\mkern 2.0mu}(\mathrm{squashed})}=\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{3}\sqrt{\frac{5^{5}}{3^{7}}}\,N^{3/2}.

(238)

Note that the latter is about
20{\mkern 2.0mu}\%
larger than the former, consistent with the above assertion that the RG-flow, if it exists, goes from squashed in the UV to round in the IR.

It would also be interesting to investigate if the marginal operators found in this paper can be used to argue holographically for a manifold of non-supersymmetric dual CFTs, in line with
Witten:2001ua
, which requires computing the
\beta
functions of the marginal operators.
However, no solid conclusions can be drawn without incorporating
1/N
or finite
N
effects, which currently seems out of reach.

The methods and results of this paper can also be used to analyse compactifications on
S^{7}/Z_{k}
. Some
AdS/CFT
aspects in this
context are discussed in, e.g.,
Ooguri:2008dk
;
Murugan:2016aty
.

From the results of this paper it is clear that all values of
E_{0}
in the squashed vacua are of the form

E_{0}=\biggl(\frac{3}{2}\pm A\biggr)+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+X}},\qquad X=1,\,9,\,49,\,\text{or}\,81,

(239)

with a constant
A
that is also restricted to a small number of special rational values. The simplicity of this result is striking,
especially in view of how the various values of
E_{0}
arose, potentially containing three layers of nested square roots,
one each coming from the operator eigenvalues, the mass formulas and finally from the formulas for
E_{0}(s)
as functions of
M
or
M^{2}
.
Even more intriguing is the fact that also the operator eigenvalue spectra
are given by similar formulas, either with or without a square root term.
From
Section

3
we see that the general forms of the eigenvalues of operators quadratic in derivatives are

\kappa^{2}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}(20C_{g}+B),\qquad\kappa^{2}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\Bigl(20C_{g}+\tilde{A}\pm\tilde{B}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+X}}\Bigr),

(240)

while for linear operators, the eigenvalues are all of the form

\kappa=\frac{m}{3}\Bigl(\pm\hat{A}\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+X}}\Bigr).

(241)

It is interesting to note, however, that all eigenvalues of the form
\kappa^{2}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}(20C_{g}+\tilde{A}\pm\tilde{B}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+X}})
can be
expressed as a square minus a constant term proportional to
m^{2}
which immediately eliminates the square root in the corresponding formula for
M^{2}
,
as was noticed already in
Ekhammar:2021gsg
. In fact, looking at the table of mass matrices in
Section

2
, we realise that the same phenomenon will
appear when using the mass formulas in the expressions for
E_{0}
. The reason behind the universal simple results for
E_{0}
is then clear.
This might nevertheless point towards the existence of a more direct derivation of
E_{0}
.

In this context we should mention that the expressions for

E_{0}
for the various supermultiplets was recently derived by entirely different methods in
Duboeuf:2022mam
, giving in addition a refined version of the
results in terms of a universal formula for
E_{0}
. Interestingly enough, these methods do not involve solving the operator equations on the squashed seven-sphere but instead rely on ExFT methods.
The results of this paper partially overlap with those of
Duboeuf:2022mam
, in particular, the
E_{0}
results that were derived explicitly for finitely many levels in
Duboeuf:2022mam
.
Note also that the many ambiguities in the choice of Dirichlet or Neumann boundary conditions we have found are not discussed in
Duboeuf:2022mam
.

We end with an observation concerning the degeneracies mentioned above. There are three pairs of identical supermultiplets,
all containing a scalar sector governed by
\Delta_{L}
, one pair of
1^{+}
and two pairs of Wess–Zumino multiplets.
We know from explicit calculations of the spectrum of
\Delta_{2}
-eigenmodes in
Section

4
, which gives also
E_{0}
,
that an infinite set of pairs of different eigenmodes (with
r=p
or
r=p\pm 2
) with identical isometry irreps appear with the same eigenvalues and, thus, also the same
E_{0}
for the corresponding fields. We do not have an
explanation for this feature but hope to come back to it in a future publication.

Instead, we point out some facts related to the operator
\Delta_{L}
which is involved in all these cases.
It is known from the work of House and Micu
House:2004pm
that
\Delta_{L}
is related to a first order differential operator, here denoted as
Q_{L}
, by

\Delta_{L}-3m^{2}=(Q_{L}+m)^{2},

(242)

which implies

M^{2}(0^{+(2)})=\Delta_{L}-4m^{4}=(Q_{L}+m)^{2}-m^{2}\ \implies\ E_{0}(0^{+(2)},Q_{L})=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\biggl|\frac{Q_{L}}{m}+1\biggr|.

(243)

Note that this seems to imply that
\Delta_{L}\geq 3m^{2}
which, however, must be supplemented by a precise statement about which modes of the two
operators the House–Micu equation is valid for. This is a rather non-trivial issue as we will indicate below.

One might interpret this to mean that the three known eigenvalues of
\Delta_{L}
could be used twice, that is, that we should be able to use
the positive and negative parts of the spectrum of
Q_{L}
when taking the square root of the House–Micu formula.
Thus we consider

\displaystyle\Delta_{L}^{(1)}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}(20C_{g}+36)

\displaystyle\implies Q_{L}=-m\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+9}},

(244)

\displaystyle\Delta_{L}^{(2)_{+}}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\Bigl(20C_{g}+32+4\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\implies Q_{L}=-m\pm\biggl(\frac{2}{3}m+\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\biggr),

(245)

\displaystyle\Delta_{L}^{(2)_{-}}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\Bigl(20C_{g}+32-4\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\implies Q_{L}=-m\pm\biggl|-\frac{2}{3}m+\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\biggr|.

(246)

However, this interpretation has a number of problems. First,
Q_{L}=-m\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+9}}
is not a possible set of
eigenvalues for an operator like
Q
acting on transverse three-forms as we saw in
Section

3
.
One issue here is that although the irrep
{\bm{2}7}
describes both
the symmetric second rank tensors and part of the three-forms, the transversality conditions act differently on these tensors making the House–Micu formula difficult to apply.
For instance, at least one of the sets of suggested
Q_{L}
-eigenvalues in
Equation

244
could instead be relevant for non-transverse three-form modes.
Furthermore, only one of the two signs in
Equations

245
and
246
give existing
Q
-eigenvalues and these are precisely the eigenvalues of
Q
that correspond to eigenmodes with only a
\bm{27}
-part and that are used by the pseudo-scalars in the supermultiplets using
\Delta_{L}^{(2)_{\pm}}
, i.e., WZ3–6.
So,
Q
does not resolve the analogous degeneracies of
\Delta_{3}
and since these
Q
-eigenvalues, which should also be
Q_{L}
-eigenvalues, have cross diagrams that already cover all isometry irreps of these supermultiplets, it seems like
Q_{L}
cannot resolve the degeneracies either.

Appendix A
Conventions on octonions, gamma-matrices and
Sp_{1}^{C}
Killing vectors

The octonions are elements of a non-associative algebra spanned by a real unit
1
and octonionic imaginary units
o_{a}
, where
a=1,\ldots,7
, with multiplication

o_{a}o_{b}=-\delta_{ab}+a_{ab}{}^{c}o_{c},

(247)

where
a_{abd}
(index lowered by
\delta_{cd}
) are the completely antisymmetric octonionic structure constants with independent components

a_{abc}=1\quad\text{for }abc=123,257,365,437,761,642,415,

(248)

in a particular basis.
By splitting the index as
a=(\hat{i},0,i)
, where
\hat{i}=(\hat{1},\hat{2},\hat{3})=(1,2,3)
and
i=(5,6,7)
, the non-zero components of
a_{abc}
can conveniently be summarised as follows
29
29

29

The indices
i,j,k
should be shifted to
(1,2,3)
to make sense of
Equation

249
.

a_{\hat{i}\hat{j}\hat{k}}=\epsilon_{ijk},\qquad a_{ij\hat{k}}=-\epsilon_{ijk},\qquad a_{0i\hat{j}}=-\delta_{ij},

(249)

in terms of the standard totally antisymmetric
\epsilon_{ijk}
and the Kronecker delta
\delta_{ij}
.
The Hodge dual of
a_{abc}
, denoted
c_{abcd}
, is

c_{abcd}=\frac{1}{6}\epsilon_{abcdefg}{\mkern 2.0mu}a^{efg},

(250)

where the seven-dimensional epsilon tensor is totally antisymmetric with
\epsilon_{1234567}=1
. This implies
that
c_{abcd}
has non-zero components

c_{0ijk}=\epsilon_{ijk},\qquad c_{0\hat{i}\hat{j}k}=-\epsilon_{ijk},\qquad c_{\hat{i}\hat{j}}{}^{kl}=-2\delta_{ij}^{kl}.

(251)

It is sometimes convenient to express the seven-dimensional gamma-matrices
\Gamma_{a}

in terms of
a_{abc}
,
c_{abcd}
and the Killing spinor of the squashed seven-sphere,
\eta
, satisfying
30
30

30

The octonions can be identified with
Spin(7)
spinors and the gamma-matrices
\Gamma_{a}
realised through multiplication by
o_{a}
.
G_{2}
is the automorphism group of the octonions or, equivalently, the subgroup of
Spin(7)
leaving the unit octonion
1
, identified with the spinor
\eta
, invariant. See, e.g.,
Karlsson:2021oxd
for more details on this perspective and how to derive some of the following relations using it.

D_{a}\eta=-\frac{i}{2}m{\mkern 2.0mu}\Gamma_{a}\eta,\qquad\bar{\eta}\eta=1.

(252)

The octonionic conventions above lead then to the relations

a_{abc}=i\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{abc}\eta,\qquad c_{abcd}=-\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{abcd}\eta,

(253)

which are consistent with the gamma-matrices satisfying their defining relations

\{\Gamma_{a},\Gamma_{b}\}=2\delta_{ab},\qquad\Gamma_{abcdefg}=i\epsilon_{abcdefg}\bf{1}.

(254)

The fact that the squashed seven-sphere is a
weak

G_{2}
manifold is then encoded in the equation

D_{a}a_{bcd}=m{\mkern 2.0mu}c_{abcd},

(255)

which is a direct consequence of the above relations with the octonionic structure constants and the Killing spinor equation.

The consistency of these conventions can be verified by explicitly writing out the seven
8\times 8
gamma-matrices, with spinor indices
A,B,C,\ldots
taking the eight values
A=(\hat{0},a)
, as follows:
31
31

31

Note that vector
a,b,c,...
and spinor
A,B,C,...
indices
are raised and lowered by
\delta_{ab}
and
\delta_{AB}
(and their inverses), respectively.

(\Gamma_{a})_{B}{}^{C}\colon\qquad(\Gamma_{a})_{b}{}^{c}=ia_{ab}{}^{c},\quad(\Gamma_{a})_{b}{}^{\hat{0}}=-i\delta_{ab}.

(256)

Note that all seven
\Gamma_{a}
are purely imaginary and antisymmetric and that they satisfy
Equation

254
.

To derive and simplify the components of
\Gamma_{ab}
and
\Gamma_{abc}
we need some structure constant identities.
We list these and, for the convenience of the reader, some other structure constant identities that are used heavily throughout the paper.
We have

\displaystyle a_{abc}{\mkern 2.0mu}a^{cde}=2\delta_{ab}^{de}+c_{ab}{}^{de},

(257)

\displaystyle a_{abc}{\mkern 2.0mu}a^{bcd}=6\delta_{a}^{d},

(258)

\displaystyle a_{abc}{\mkern 2.0mu}a^{abc}=42.

(259)

The analogous identities involving
c_{abcd}
are

\displaystyle c_{abcd}{\mkern 2.0mu}c^{defg}=a_{abc}{\mkern 2.0mu}a^{efg}-9c_{[ab}{}^{[ef}\delta_{c]}^{g]}-6\delta_{abc}^{efg},

(260)

\displaystyle c_{abcd}{\mkern 2.0mu}c^{cdef}=8\delta_{ab}^{ef}+2c_{ab}{}^{ef},

(261)

\displaystyle c_{abcd}{\mkern 2.0mu}c^{bcde}=-24\delta_{a}^{e},

(262)

\displaystyle c_{abcd}{\mkern 2.0mu}c^{abcd}=168.

(263)

Some identities involving contractions between the
a_{abc}
and
c_{abcd}
structure constants are:

\displaystyle c_{abcd}{\mkern 2.0mu}a^{def}=6a_{[ab}{}^{[e}\delta_{c]}^{f]},

(264)

\displaystyle c_{abcd}{\mkern 2.0mu}a^{cde}=4a_{ab}{}^{e},

(265)

\displaystyle c_{abcd}{\mkern 2.0mu}a^{bcd}=0.

(266)

Two other identities that have been used in this work are

\displaystyle\delta_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4!}a_{a}{}^{cd}a_{b}{}^{ef}c_{cdef},

(267)

\displaystyle a_{[ab}{}^{[d}a_{c]}{}^{ef]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}a_{abc}a^{def}-2c_{[ab}{}^{[de}\delta_{c]}^{f]}.

(268)

These and the following identities may be verified directly by using the components in the basis given above. By using the Fierz identity of the
G_{2}
-invariant
\eta
,

\Gamma_{a}\eta\bar{\eta}\Gamma^{a}=1-\eta\bar{\eta},

(269)

one can also prove
Equations

257
–
267
more elegantly.

Using the octonionic definition of the gamma-matrices

(\Gamma_{a})_{B}{}^{C}
above we can express also the antisymmetric matrices
(\Gamma_{ab})_{CD}
this way. We find

(\Gamma_{ab})_{C}{}^{D}\colon\qquad(\Gamma_{ab})^{cd}=-c_{ab}{}^{cd}+2\delta_{ab}^{cd},\quad(\Gamma_{ab})_{c}{}^{\hat{0}}=-a_{abc}.

(270)

Finally, for the symmetric matrices
(\Gamma_{abc})_{DE}
we have

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{abc})_{D}{}^{E}\colon\qquad

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{abc})^{de}=-ic_{[ab}{}^{ef}a_{c]f}{}^{d}-ia_{[ab}{}^{e}\delta_{c]}^{d}-2ia_{[ab}{}^{d}\delta_{c]}^{e},

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{abc})_{d}{}^{\hat{0}}=-ic_{abcd},

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{abc})_{\hat{0}}{}^{\hat{0}}=-ia_{abc}.

(271)

The first one of these expressions can be simplified further using the identity (note the order of the indices)

c_{[ab|d|}{}^{f}a_{c]f}{}^{e}=6\bigl(\delta_{[a}^{[c^{\prime}}a_{bd]}{}^{e]}\delta_{cc^{\prime}}\bigr)\big|_{[abc]}.

(272)

Then, the required symmetry property in
de
becomes clear:

(\Gamma_{abc})^{de}=i\bigl(a_{abc}\delta^{de}-6\delta^{(d}_{[a}a_{bc]}{}^{e)}\bigr).

(273)

Finally, we see that
\operatorname{Tr}(\Gamma^{abc})=0
as it must.

The relations above show that this representation of the gamma-matrices is consistent with the definition of the structure constants
a_{abc}
in
terms of the Killing spinor
\eta
.
To clarify this further we can solve the Killing spinor equation and get an explicit form of this spinor. We know from the left-squashed
S^{7}
compactification that the
holonomy is
G_{2}
and hence that the Killing spinor equation has one solution. Normalising it to
\bar{\eta}\eta=1

we for now assume that, in the above basis,
\eta
has only one non-zero real constant component. Then

\displaystyle D_{a}\eta_{B}=-\frac{i}{2}m(\Gamma_{a})_{B}{}^{C}\eta_{C}\colon

\displaystyle B=b\colon\quad

\displaystyle\partial_{a}\eta_{b}+\frac{1}{4}\omega_{ade}\bigl((\Gamma^{de})_{b}{}^{c}\eta_{c}+(\Gamma^{de})_{b}{}^{\hat{0}}\eta_{\hat{0}}\bigr)=-\frac{i}{2}m\bigl((\Gamma_{a})_{b}{}^{c}\eta_{c}+(\Gamma_{a})_{b}{}^{{\hat{0}}}\eta_{\hat{0}}\bigr)

\displaystyle B=\hat{0}\colon\quad

\displaystyle\partial_{a}\eta_{\hat{0}}+\frac{1}{4}\omega_{ade}(\Gamma^{de})_{\hat{0}}{}^{c}\eta_{c}=-\frac{i}{2}m(\Gamma_{a})_{\hat{0}}{}^{c}\eta_{c}

(274)

It is clear from these two equations that a spinor
\eta
with only a constant non-zero component
\eta_{\hat{0}}
has a chance to be a solution. Assuming this to be the case, the second equation is solved and the first becomes the single
condition on the spin connection

\frac{1}{4}\omega_{a}{}^{de}a_{bde}=\frac{m}{2}\delta_{ab}\quad\implies\quad\hat{\omega}_{abc}=\frac{m}{3}a_{abc},

(275)

where the hat indicates that there might be (and are in case of the squashed seven-sphere) other terms that vanish when contracted with
a_{abc}
this way.
This condition is in fact satisfied but the easiest way to verify the assumed form of the Killing spinor is instead to consider the
integrability condition

W_{ab}{}^{cd}\Gamma_{cd}\eta=0,

(276)

where
W_{ab}{}^{cd}
is the Weyl tensor and the only linearly independent solution is

\eta_{A}=(1,0,....,0).

(277)

Note that this explicit form of
\eta
and the gamma-matrices is consistent with
\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{abc}\eta=-ia_{abc}
.
One can check that this
\eta
solves not only the integrability condition but also the Killing spinor equation on the left-squashed seven-sphere but not on the right-squashed one
Awada:1982pk
;
Duff:1983ajq
.
That the integrability condition
Equation

276
has exactly one independent solution is equivalent to the fact that the holonomy of the Killing spinor equation is
G_{2}
, i.e.,
\frac{1}{4}W_{ab}{}^{cd}\Gamma_{cd}
generates the Lie algreba
g_{2}
.

There are also identities where structure constants are contracted with the
Sp_{1}^{C}
Killing vector components

s_{a}{}^{j}=(s_{\hat{i}}{}^{j},\;s_{0}{}^{j},\;s_{i}{}^{j})=\biggl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\delta^{j}_{i},\;0,\;0\biggr).

(278)

The
Sp_{1}^{C}
Killing vectors are used heavily
in the mode calculations in
Section

4
. From the explicit realisation of
a_{abc}
and
c_{abcd}
given above one finds the following identities:

\displaystyle a_{a}{}^{bc}s_{b}{}^{i}s_{c}{}^{j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\epsilon^{ij}{}_{k}s_{a}{}^{k},

(279)

\displaystyle c_{ab}{}^{cd}s_{c}{}^{i}s_{d}{}^{j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\epsilon^{ij}{}_{k}a_{ab}{}^{c}s_{c}{}^{k}-2s_{[a}{}^{i}s_{b]}{}^{j},

(280)

\displaystyle s_{[a|}{}^{i}s^{d}{}_{i}a_{d|bc]}=\frac{1}{15}a_{abc}+\frac{2\sqrt{5}}{3}\epsilon_{ijk}s_{a}{}^{i}s_{b}{}^{j}s_{c}{}^{k},

(281)

In addition, there is an important derivative relation given in
Nilsson:1983ru
which in our notation reads

D_{a}s_{b}{}^{i}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{5}}a_{ab}{}^{c}s_{c}{}^{i}-3\epsilon^{i}{}_{jk}s_{a}{}^{j}s_{b}{}^{k}.

(282)

Appendix B
G_{2}
and
H
projection operators and Casimirs

Projection operators picking out the various
G_{2}
-irreps occurring in the decompositions of two-forms, three-forms, spinors and vector-spinors on
S^{7}
under
Spin(7)\rightarrow G_{2}
are, for the convenience of the reader, given below (the other
so(7)
-irreps of interest are also
g_{2}
-irreps and so do not need to be decomposed).
For spinors, one- and two-forms, we also give the projection operators onto the
H
-irreps occurring when decomposing
Spin(7)\rightarrow G_{2}\rightarrow H
.
This is relevant for
Section

4
, in particular
Section

4.2
since
C_{h}
is not proportional to the identity on
\bm{14}_{g_{2}}\subset\bm{21}_{so(7)}
.
In
Section

B.2
, we give our conventions for the Casimirs of the various groups.

B.1
Projection operators

One-forms

The one-form irrep
\bm{7}
of
so(7)
does not decompose when breaking
so(7)
to
g_{2}
.
However, when breaking to
h=sp_{1}^{A}\oplus sp_{1}^{B+C}\subset g_{2}
, it decomposes as
\bm{7}\rightarrow(0,2)\oplus(1,1)
.
Since
s_{a}{}^{i}s^{b}{}_{i}
is a
H
-singlet and has rank three when seen as an operator acting on one-forms, we have

(P_{(0,2)})_{a}{}^{b}=5s_{a}{}^{i}s^{b}{}_{i},

(283)

where we have fixed the coefficient by demanding
P_{(0,2)}{}^{2}=P_{(0,2)}
.
It follows that the projector onto
(1,1)
is

(P_{(1,1)})_{a}{}^{b}=\delta_{a}^{b}-5s_{a}{}^{i}s^{b}{}_{i}.

(284)

Two-forms

The two-form irrep
\bm{21}
decomposes into
\bm{7}\oplus\bm{14}
under
g_{2}
.
Since
a_{a}{}^{bc}
can be viewed as an intertwiner
\bm{21}\to\bm{7}
, we have

\displaystyle(P_{7})_{ab}{}^{cd}=\frac{1}{6}a_{ab}{}^{e}a^{cde}=\frac{1}{6}(2\delta_{ab}^{cd}+c_{ab}{}^{cd}),

(285)

\displaystyle(P_{14})_{ab}{}^{cd}=(1-P_{7})_{ab}{}^{cd}=\frac{1}{6}(4\delta_{ab}^{cd}-c_{ab}{}^{cd}).

(286)

From this, we see that

c_{ab}{}^{cd}=(4P_{7}-2P_{14})_{ab}{}^{cd},

(287)

which is often useful in computations.

When restricting to
H
, the
\bm{7}
decomposes as described in the one-form case above and the
\bm{14}
decomposes into
(0,2)\oplus(2,0)\oplus(1,3)
.
The projection operators projecting onto the
H
-irreps in
\bm{7}
can be constructed using
a_{abc}
and the projectors from the one-form case.
For instance, we see that (c.f.
Equation

283
)

(P_{(0,2)\subset\bm{7}})_{ab}{}^{cd}=\frac{5}{6}a_{ab}{}^{e}s_{e}{}^{i}s^{f}{}_{\!i}{\mkern 2.0mu}a_{f}{}^{cd}=75(P_{7}\varsigma P_{7})_{ab}{}^{cd},

(288)

where

\varsigma_{ab}{}^{cd}=s_{[a|}{}^{i}s^{[c|}{}_{i}{\mkern 2.0mu}s_{|b]}{}^{j}s^{|d]}_{j}

(289)

is a useful building block for constructing the projectors.
One can verify that all four
g_{2}
-blocks of
\varsigma_{ab}{}^{cd}
, viewed as a linear operator acting on two-forms, that appear when decomposing
\bm{21}\to\bm{7}\oplus\bm{14}
have rank three.
Since the
7\times 7
block is proportional to the projector
(P_{(0,2)\subset\bm{7}})_{ab}{}^{cd}
, the off-diagonal blocks intertwine
(0,2)\subset\bm{7}
and
(0,2)\subset\bm{14}
and the
14\times 14
block is proportional to
(P_{(0,2)\subset\bm{14}})_{ab}{}^{cd}
.
Working out the normalisation, we find

(P_{(0,2)\subset\bm{14}})_{ab}{}^{cd}=\frac{75}{2}(P_{14}\varsigma P_{14})_{ab}{}^{cd}.

(290)

Another useful building block is (c.f.
Equation

284
)

(\sigma-5\varsigma)_{ab}{}^{cd}=(\delta_{[a|}^{[c|}-5s_{[a|}{}^{i}s^{[c|}{}_{i})s_{|b]}{}^{j}s^{|d]}_{j},\quad\text{where}\quad\sigma_{ab}{}^{cd}=\delta_{[a|}^{[c|}s_{|b]}{}^{i}s^{|d]}_{i}.

(291)

The off-diagonal
g_{2}
-blocks of
(\sigma-5\varsigma)_{ab}{}^{cd}
vanish while the
7\times 7
and
14\times 14
blocks have ranks four and eight which are the dimensions of
(1,1)\subset\bm{7}
and
(1,3)\subset\bm{14}
, respectively.
Hence, by demanding that the projectors square to themselves to fix the normalisation, we find

(P_{(1,1)\subset\bm{7}})_{ab}{}^{cd}=10P_{7}(\sigma-5\varsigma)_{ab}{}^{cd}P_{7},\quad(P_{(1,3)\subset\bm{14}})_{ab}{}^{cd}=10P_{14}(\sigma-5\varsigma)_{ab}{}^{cd}P_{14}.

(292)

The last projector is simply

(P_{(2,0)\subset\bm{14}})_{ab}{}^{cd}=(P_{14}-P_{(0,2)\subset\bm{14}}-P_{(1,3)\subset\bm{14}})_{ab}{}^{cd}.

(293)

These projection operators can be used to get an expression for the Weyl tensor.
Specifically, by using
Equation

38
on two-forms we find that

W_{ab}{}^{cd}=\frac{1}{5}(4P_{14}+8P_{(0,2)\subset\bm{14}}-10P_{(1,3)\subset\bm{14}})_{ab}{}^{cd}\\
=\frac{1}{5}P_{14}(4-100\sigma+800\varsigma)_{ab}{}^{cd}P_{14}.

(294)

In computations, we sometimes find it useful to rewrite this as

W_{ab}{}^{cd}=\frac{4}{5}\delta_{ab}^{cd}-20\sigma_{ab}{}^{cd}+160\varsigma_{ab}{}^{cd}+12(P_{7}\sigma)_{ab}{}^{cd}-120(P_{7}\varsigma)_{ab}{}^{cd}-60(\varsigma P_{7})_{ab}{}^{cd}.

(295)

Three-forms

The three-form irrep
\bm{35}
of
so(7)
decomposes into
\bm{1}\oplus\bm{7}\oplus\bm{27}
when restricted to
g_{2}
.
Clearly,
a_{abc}
and
c_{a}{}^{bcd}
can be viewed as intertwiners from three-forms to scalars and one-forms, respectively, which allows us to easily construct the projection operators onto
\bm{1}
and
\bm{7}
.
The projector onto the
\bm{27}
is then obtained by subtracting the previous two from the identity.
Fixing the normalisations through
P^{2}=P
, this gives

\displaystyle(P_{1})_{abc}{}^{def}=\frac{1}{42}a_{abc}a^{def},

(296)

\displaystyle(P_{7})_{abc}{}^{def}=-\frac{1}{24}c_{abc}{}^{g}c_{g}{}^{def}=\frac{1}{24}(6\delta_{abc}^{def}+9\delta_{[a}^{[d}c_{bc]}{}^{ef]}-a_{abc}a^{def}),

(297)

\displaystyle(P_{27})_{abc}{}^{def}=(1-P_{1}-P_{7})_{abc}{}^{def}=\frac{1}{56}(42\delta_{abc}^{def}-21\delta_{[a}^{[d}c_{bc]}{}^{ef]}+a_{abc}a^{def}).

(298)

Spin 1/2

The spinor
\bm{8}
of
so(7)
splits into a singlet
\bm{1}
, the Killing spinor, and a
\bm{7}
when restricted to
g_{2}
.
The projection operators are

P_{1}=\eta\bar{\eta},\qquad P_{7}=\Gamma_{a}\eta\bar{\eta}\Gamma^{a},

(299)

where
\eta
is the normalised Killing spinor (
\bar{\eta}\eta=1
) discussed in detail in
Appendix

A
.

The projectors can be written in terms of gamma-matrices as

P_{1}=\frac{1}{8}+\frac{i}{48}a_{abc}\Gamma^{abc},\qquad P_{7}=\frac{7}{8}-\frac{i}{48}a_{abc}\Gamma^{abc},

(300)

where we have used that
a_{abc}=i\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{abc}\eta
.
This is seen by computing the traces
\operatorname{Tr}(P\Gamma^{(n)})
where
n=0,1,2,3
indicates the number of indices on
\Gamma
.
The above proves the Fierz identity
\Gamma_{a}\eta\bar{\eta}\Gamma^{a}=1-\eta\bar{\eta}
which is nothing but
P_{1}+P_{7}=1
.

Lastly, we note that the projectors onto the
H
-irreps appearing in the decomposition of the spinor under
so(7)\rightarrow h
are immediately obtained from the above as

P_{(0,2)}=\Gamma^{a}\eta(5s_{a}{}^{i}s^{b}{}_{i})\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{b},\qquad P_{(1,1)}=\Gamma^{a}\eta(\delta_{a}^{b}-5s_{a}{}^{i}s^{b}{}_{i})\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{b}.

(301)

Spin 3/2

Gamma-traceless vector-spinors, or spin-3/2 fields, transform in the
\bm{48}
of
so(7)
, which decomposes into
\bm{7}\oplus\bm{14}\oplus\bm{27}
under
so(7)\rightarrow g_{2}
.
From
Equations

107
–
110
one can read off the projection operators (with supressed spinor indices)

\displaystyle(P_{7})_{a}{}^{b}=\frac{1}{6}(6\delta_{a}^{b}\eta+ia_{a}{}^{bc}\Gamma_{c}\eta)\bar{\eta},

(302)

\displaystyle(P_{14})_{a}{}^{b}=-(P_{14})_{ac}{}^{db}\Gamma^{c}\eta\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{d},

(303)

\displaystyle(P_{27})_{a}{}^{b}=\delta_{(a}^{b}\Gamma^{c}\eta\bar{\eta}\Gamma_{c)},

(304)

where
(P_{14})_{ac}{}^{db}
on the second line is the two-form projection operator.

B.2
Casimir conventions

In this subsection, we give our conventions for the Casimirs of the groups appearing in the analysis.
First, the isometry group (
G=Sp_{2}\times Sp_{1}^{C}
) Casimir in the master formula for
\Delta
,
Equation

40
, is

C_{g}(p,q;r)=-T_{A}T^{A}=2C_{sp_{2}}(p,q)+3C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}(r),

(305)

where
(p,q)
and
(r)
are the Dynkin labels of
sp_{2}
and
sp_{1}^{C}
, respectively,
C_{sp_{2}}
and
C_{sp_{1}}
are given in
Table

14
and, as in
Section

2
, we raise and lower
G
-indices using
-\kappa_{AB}/6
and its inverse, where
\kappa_{AB}
is the negative definite Cartan–Killing form.
The Casimir of the isotropy group,
H=Sp_{1}^{A}\times Sp_{1}^{B+C}
, is

C_{h}=-T_{i}T^{i}=2C_{sp_{1}^{A}}+\frac{6}{5}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{B+C}}},

(306)

where
H
-indices are raised and lowered using the restriction of
-\kappa_{AB}/6
to
h
and its inverse.

The Casimirs of
Spin(7)
and
G_{2}
also appear in
Equation

40
.
These are defined by

C_{so(7)}=-\Sigma_{ab}\Sigma^{ab},\qquad C_{g_{2}}=-(P_{14})_{abcd}\Sigma^{ab}\Sigma^{cd}.

(307)

Formulas for their eigenvalues on irreps in terms of Dynkin labels are provided in
Table

14
.

Casimir

Adjoint

C_{sp_{1}}(r)=\frac{1}{4}{\mkern 2.0mu}r(r+2)

C_{sp_{1}}(2)=2

C_{sp_{2}}(p,q)=\frac{1}{4}{\mkern 2.0mu}p(p+2q+4)+\frac{1}{2}{\mkern 2.0mu}q(q+3)

C_{sp_{2}}(2,0)=3

C_{g_{2}}(p,q)=\frac{1}{3}{\mkern 2.0mu}p(p+3q+5)+q(q+3)

C_{g_{2}}(0,1)=4

C_{so(7)}(p,q,r)=\frac{1}{2}{\mkern 2.0mu}p(p+2q+r+5)+q(q+r+4)+\frac{3}{8}{\mkern 2.0mu}r(r+6)

C_{so(7)}(0,1,0)=5

Table 14:
Casimir eigenvalues on irreps specified by Dynkin labels with the adjoint representation given explicitly.

Appendix C
Non-transverse modes and two-form mode scalar products

In this appendix we give the non-transverse eigenmodes of

and

and

.

upon comparison.

(309)

and norm

\|\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{(2)}\phi\|^{2}=C_{g}^{\phi}\|\phi\|^{2}.

(310)

Hence, this non-transverse eigenmode exists for
(p,q;r)=(p,q;p)
with
(p,q)\neq(0,0)
corresponding to the cross diagram in

.

and

(0,2)
and

.

when

.

This provides the first consistency check of the type described above.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{(4)i}_{ab}&=\frac{1}{20}\Bigl(15\Delta C-13\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\Bigr)\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}_{ab}+\frac{3}{2\sqrt{5}}\Bigl(13\mp\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\Bigr)\mathcal{Y}^{(3)i}_{ab}\\

(311)

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}^{(4)_{\pm}}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\Bigl(20C_{g}+14\pm 2\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\Bigr).

(312)

To compute the norms of these non-transverse eigenmodes, and also for the norms of the transverse eigenmodes in

leading to

\mathcal{Y}^{(\text{1--7})}_{ab}

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\phi\rangle=6C_{g}^{\phi}\|\phi\|^{2},

(313)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}s_{i}\phi\rangle=6C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\|\phi\|^{2},

(314)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(3)i}s_{i}\phi\rangle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\|\phi\|^{2},

(315)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}s_{i}\phi\rangle=-\frac{3}{\sqrt{5}}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\|\phi\|^{2},

(316)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(5)i}s_{i}\phi\rangle=4\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}s_{i}\phi\rangle,

(317)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(6)i}s_{i}\phi\rangle=\frac{27}{10}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\|\phi\|^{2},

(318)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(7)ij}s_{ij}\phi\rangle=-\frac{1}{6}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\bigl(4C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}-3\bigr)\|\phi\|^{2},

(319)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(2)j}\varphi\rangle=\frac{6}{5}\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle,

(320)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(3)j}\varphi\rangle=\frac{2}{5\sqrt{5}}\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle,

(321)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(4)j}\varphi\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\langle\phi,\epsilon^{ijk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle,

(322)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(5)j}\varphi\rangle=4\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(4)j}\varphi\rangle,

(323)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(6)j}\varphi\rangle=\frac{1}{5}C_{g}^{\phi}\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle+\langle\phi,s^{ij}\varphi\rangle,

(324)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(7)jk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle=-\frac{1}{10}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle-\frac{1}{12}\langle\phi,\epsilon^{ijk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle+\frac{1}{30}\langle\phi,s^{ij}\varphi\rangle,

(325)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(3)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(3)j}\varphi\rangle=\frac{2}{25}\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle,

(326)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(3)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(4)j}\varphi\rangle=\frac{1}{5}\langle\phi,\epsilon^{ijk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle,

(327)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(3)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(5)j}\varphi\rangle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(4)j}\varphi\rangle-2\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(3)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(4)j}\varphi\rangle,

(328)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(3)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(6)j}\varphi\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\langle\phi,s^{ij}\varphi\rangle,

(329)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(3)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(7)jk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle=-\frac{1}{10\sqrt{5}}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle-\frac{1}{12\sqrt{5}}\langle\phi,\epsilon^{ijk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle+\frac{1}{30\sqrt{5}}\langle\phi,s^{ij}\varphi\rangle,

(330)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(4)j}\varphi\rangle=\frac{1}{10}C_{g}^{\phi}\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\phi,\epsilon^{ijk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi,s^{ij}\varphi\rangle,

(331)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(5)j}\varphi\rangle=-\frac{2}{5}\langle\phi,\epsilon^{ijk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle,

(332)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(6)j}\varphi\rangle&=\frac{1}{20\sqrt{5}}\bigl(C_{g}^{\phi}-30C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\bigr)\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle+\frac{1}{8\sqrt{5}}\bigl(4C_{g}^{\phi}-9\bigr)\langle\phi,\epsilon^{ijk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle\\
&\phantom{{}={}}-\frac{11}{4\sqrt{5}}\langle\phi,s^{ij}\varphi\rangle,\end{aligned}

(333)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(7)jk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle&=-\frac{3}{20\sqrt{5}}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle-\frac{1}{12\sqrt{5}}\bigl(3C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}-2\bigr)\langle\phi,\epsilon^{ijk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle\\
&\phantom{{}={}}-\frac{11}{30\sqrt{5}}\langle\phi,s^{ij}\varphi\rangle,\end{aligned}

(334)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(5)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(5)j}\varphi\rangle=8\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(4)j}\varphi\rangle+2\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(5)j}\varphi\rangle,

(335)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(5)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(6)j}\varphi\rangle=2\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}\phi,(3P_{7}-1)\mathcal{Y}^{(6)j}\varphi\rangle,

(336)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(5)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(7)jk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle=2\langle\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}\phi,(3P_{7}-1)\mathcal{Y}^{(7)jk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle,

(337)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\mathcal{Y}^{(6)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(6)j}\varphi\rangle&=\frac{1}{200}C_{g}^{\phi}\bigl(20C_{g}^{\phi}-39\bigr)\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle-\frac{1}{80}\bigl(36C_{g}^{\phi}-171\bigr)\langle\phi,\epsilon^{ijk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle\\
&\phantom{{}={}}+\frac{1}{40}\bigl(20C_{g}^{\phi}-3\bigr)\langle\phi,s^{ij}\varphi\rangle,\end{aligned}

(338)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\mathcal{Y}^{(6)i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(7)jk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle&=-\frac{1}{200}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\bigl(10C_{g}^{\phi}-47\bigr)\langle\phi,\delta^{ij}\varphi\rangle\\
&\phantom{{}={}}-\frac{1}{120}\bigl(5C_{g}^{\phi}-19C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}+7\bigr)\langle\phi,\epsilon^{ijk}s_{k}\varphi\rangle\\
&\phantom{{}={}}+\frac{1}{300}\bigl(5C_{g}^{\phi}-100C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}+214\bigr)\langle\phi,s^{ij}\varphi\rangle,\end{aligned}

(339)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\mathcal{Y}^{(7)ij}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{(7)}_{kl}\varphi\rangle&=\frac{1}{50}\bigl(C_{g}^{\phi}-5C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\bigr)\langle\phi,\delta^{\{i}_{\{k}\delta^{j\}}_{l\}}\varphi\rangle+\frac{9}{100}\langle\phi,\delta^{\{i}_{\{k}\epsilon_{l\}}{}^{j\}m}s_{m}\varphi\rangle\\
&\phantom{{}={}}-\frac{1}{10}\langle\phi,\delta^{\{i}_{\{k}s_{l\}}{}^{j\}}\varphi\rangle,\end{aligned}

(340)

where

\displaystyle P_{7}\mathcal{Y}^{(6)i}_{ab}=\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}_{ab}s^{i}-\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{Y}^{(2)i}_{ab}\check{\Box}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{i}{}_{jk}\mathcal{Y}^{(2)j}_{ab}s^{k}+\frac{1}{6\sqrt{5}}\mathcal{Y}^{(4)i}_{ab}+\frac{1}{12\sqrt{5}}\mathcal{Y}^{(5)i}_{ab},

(341)

\displaystyle P_{7}\mathcal{Y}^{(7)ij}_{ab}=\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{Y}^{(2)\{i}_{ab}s^{j\}}.

(342)

To compute scalar products and norms like
\langle P_{r}\mathcal{Y}^{m}\phi,P_{r}\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}\phi\rangle
, we need to use
Equation

151
in cases where the differential operators are not
sp_{1}^{C}
-singlets.
For the
\bm{5}_{sp_{1}^{C}}
case, we need some
sp_{1}^{C}
identities, such as

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(i}{}_{kl}\delta^{j)}_{(m}\delta_{n)}^{k}\epsilon^{(m|p}{}_{r}\mathcal{Y}_{pq}s^{|n)qr}s^{l}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}Y^{ij}s^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\delta^{ij}\mathcal{Y}^{kl}s_{kl}-\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{(i}{}_{kl}\mathcal{Y}^{j)k}s^{l}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}-\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{Y}^{k(i}s_{k}{}^{j)}(7+3s^{2})+\frac{3}{2}\epsilon^{(i|k}{}_{m}\mathcal{Y}_{kl}s^{|j)lm}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Y}_{kl}s^{ijkl},

(343)

since
Equation

151
requires four successive applications of the Casimir

\displaystyle C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}\mathcal{Y}^{ij}\phi

\displaystyle=-(T_{k}T^{k}\mathcal{Y}^{ij})\phi-\mathcal{Y}^{ij}(T_{k}T^{k}\phi)-2(T_{k}\mathcal{Y}^{ij})(T^{k}\phi)

\displaystyle=\bigl(C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}+6\bigr)\mathcal{Y}^{ij}\phi-4\epsilon^{(i}{}_{kl}\mathcal{Y}^{j)k}s^{l},

(344)

where the
6
comes from
C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}(\bm{5})=6
.
This reduces the computation of
\langle P_{r}\mathcal{Y}^{m}\phi,P_{r}\mathcal{Y}^{(n)}\phi\rangle
to
Equations

313
–
340
with
\varphi
substituted by one of

\delta_{ij}\phi,\qquad\epsilon_{ijk}s^{k}\phi,\qquad s_{ij},

(345)

in the
\bm{3}_{sp_{1}^{C}}
cases (of the form
\langle\mathcal{Y}^{i}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{\prime j}\varphi\rangle
) and one of

\delta_{(i}^{(k}\delta_{j)}^{l)}\phi,\quad\epsilon_{(i}{}^{(k|m|}\delta_{j)}^{l)}s_{m}\phi,\quad\delta_{(i}^{(k}s_{j)}{}^{l)}\phi,\quad\epsilon_{(i}{}^{(k|m|}s_{j)m}{}^{l)}\phi,\quad s_{ij}{}^{kl}\phi,

(346)

in the
\bm{5}_{sp_{1}^{C}}
cases (of the form
\langle\mathcal{Y}^{ij}\phi,\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{kl}\varphi\rangle
).
Lastly, to put the above together, we need identities like
Equation

170
in the
\bm{3}_{sp_{1}^{C}}
case and, for instance,

\langle\phi,s_{l}{}^{j}s_{ij}{}^{il}\phi\rangle=-C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\biggl(\frac{5}{36}-\frac{23}{54}C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}+\frac{4}{9}\bigl(C_{\smash[t]{sp_{1}^{C}}}^{\phi}\bigr)^{2}\biggr)\|\phi\|^{2},

(347)

in the
\bm{5}_{sp_{1}^{C}}
case.

Appendix E
Derivation of the supermultiplets

In this appendix, we explain, in detail, how one can associate
E_{0}
values and cross diagrams to each of the supermultiplets as summarized in

Tables

4
,
5
and
8
.

In the process of explaining this we also obtain information of how to assign unique operator eigenvalues to the various cross diagrams, or single crosses in some special cases.
Based on the results of
Nilsson:2018lof
;
Karlsson:2021oxd
we here use supersymmetry to carry this analysis as far as possible but a
second goal is to correlate these results with the ones obtained in
Section

4
where we provide a direct connection between
cross diagrams and operator eigenvalues. This is particularly important for the novel results concerning

\Delta_{2}
which will give new important insights into the structure of supermultiplets.
Thus, we will use the notation for cross diagrams and their eigenvalues introduced in
Section

4
and in some cases compare them to the cross diagrams listed in the appendix of
Nilsson:2018lof
.
Alternatively, one can start from the results of
Sections

3
and
4
and arrive at the same results without using
Nilsson:2018lof
except for the cross diagrams of the supermultiplets only associated to mode types that were not analysed in
Section

4
, specifically the results in
Figure

8
below.

In what follows, we do not look for numerical coincidences that could give rise to exceptional supermultiplets for small
p
or
q
.
Although we do not present the details, we have verified that no such exceptions are possible when accounting for the full spectrum of supermultiplets.

Spin-2 supermultiplets

We start from the top of the list of supermultiplets,
Equations

218
–
222
above, and follow it downwards. The first one is thus the spin
2^{+}
supermultiplets

D(E_{0},2^{+}:\Delta_{0})\oplus D(E_{0}-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}:i\not{D}_{1/2})\oplus D(E_{0}+\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}:i\not{D}_{1/2})\oplus D(E_{0},1^{+}:\Delta_{2}),

(348)

where we have included the operators responsible for the different spectra. The values of
E_{0}
are computed from the operator eigenvalues
as follows: Using
Table

3
and
Equation

131
we get
M^{2}(2^{+})=\Delta_{0}
with eigenvalues
\Delta_{0}^{(1)}=\frac{20m^{2}}{9}C_{g}
which gives (see
Table

2
)

E_{0}(2^{+})=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\left(\frac{M}{m}\right)^{2}+9}=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{20}{9}C_{g}+9}=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},

(349)

applicable to all isometry irreps in the cross diagram of
\Delta_{0}
, that is,
all irreps
(p,q;r)
with
p=r\geq 0
,
q\geq 0
(see
Nilsson:2018lof
or
Figure

1
).
There is, however, a special case namely the short massless
supergravity multiplet in the irrep
(0,0;0)
. Looking more carefully at the cross diagrams for the different fields in the spin
2^{+}
supermultiplets one finds that the cross
diagrams of
Nilsson:2018lof
are related as follows
34
34

34

The signs appearing as indices on the 3/2 components refer to the sign in their energies
E_{0}\pm\frac{1}{2}
.

\displaystyle 2^{+}\colon[\Delta_{0}](1),\ (3/2)_{\scriptscriptstyle(-)}\colon[i\not{D}_{1/2}](1)=[\Delta_{0}](1),\ (3/2)_{\scriptscriptstyle(+)}\colon[i\not{D}_{1/2}](2)=[\Delta_{0}](1)-(0,0;0),

\displaystyle 1^{+}\colon[\Delta_{2}](2+3-1)=[\Delta_{0}](1)-(0,0;0),

(350)

where the bracket
[...]
enclosing the operator indicates that it refers to a cross diagram and not an eigenvalue.
Here
[i\not{D}_{1/2}](1)
refers to the first cross diagram in the list of
i\not{D}_{1/2}
in the appendix of
Nilsson:2018lof
while
[\Delta_{2}](2+3-1)
means that one should add
the
\Delta_{2}
cross diagrams number 2 and 3 and subtract diagram number 1. Thus we see that the last two fields in the supermultiplets have cross diagrams that do not contain the irrep

(0,0;0)
which therefore gives rise to a short supermultiplet, the supergravity multiplet. In particular this means that the Dirac eigenvalues are associated with cross diagrams as follows

\displaystyle i\not{D}_{1/2}(1)=\frac{m}{2}+\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}\quad\implies\quad E_{0}=\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},

(351)

\displaystyle i\not{D}_{1/2}(2)=\frac{m}{2}-\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}\quad\implies\quad E_{0}=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},

(352)

which follows from the old results of
Nilsson:1983ru
or the results in this paper.
35
35

35

Note that the sign in front of
the first term cannot be deduced from the eigenvalue calculations in
Section

3
of this paper, which are based on computing
(i{\not{D}}_{1/2})^{2}
, but follows here instead from
implementing supersymmetry. The mode calculation in
Section

4
, on the other hand, does provide the sign.

Similarly, we conclude that, in analogy with
\Delta_{0}(1)=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,20C_{g}
, we have

\Delta_{2}(2+3-1)=\frac{m^{2}}{9}(20C_{g}+72)\quad\implies\quad E_{0}(1^{+})=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},

(353)

with the same
E_{0}
as for the spin
2^{+}
component, as required by supersymmetry.

Using the notation of
Section

4
we can summarize the result as follows: The four fields in the
2^{+}
supersmultiplet make use of the following cross diagrams and eigenvalues

2^{+}(\Delta_{0}^{(1)}),\quad\frac{3}{2}(i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(1)_{+}}),\quad\frac{3}{2}(i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(1)_{-}}),\quad 1^{+}(\Delta_{2}^{(1)}),

(354)

given in
Figures

1
,
3
and
4
.
It is interesting to note that the
combination of diagrams from the appendix of
Nilsson:2018lof
appearing in
[\Delta_{2}](2+3-1)=[\Delta_{0}](1)-(0,0;0)
comes out automatically in the mode calculation
presented in
Section

4
as
\Delta_{2}^{(1)}
. This will happen many times in the following when comparing these two approaches but from now on we do not give any details on this issue.
We should mention that apart from combining entire cross diagrams it is also necessary in some cases to move columns or rows, or just single crosses, between diagrams
in
Nilsson:2018lof
in order to generate the ones that come out of the mode function analysis in
Section

4
.
This is to be expected since the analysis of
Nilsson:2018lof
only provides the mutliplicity of the isometry irreps for the various operators; the split into separate cross diagrams in
Nilsson:2018lof
is to some extent arbitrary.

Spin-3/2 supermultiplets

Turning to the next set of supermultiplets, the six spin-3/2 ones, we have

D(E_{0},\tfrac{3}{2}:i\not{D}_{1/2})\oplus D(E_{0}-\tfrac{1}{2},1^{\pm}:\Delta_{2\,\text{or}\,1})\oplus D(E_{0}+\tfrac{1}{2},1^{\mp}:\Delta_{1\,\text{or}\,2})\oplus D(E_{0},\tfrac{1}{2}:i\not{D}_{3/2}).

(355)

Here we must relate each of the two spin 1 parity assignments to three cross diagrams, a fact that follows by checking how supersymmetry works starting from the
six cross diagrams for the spin-3/2 component.
We conclude immediately that these are determined by the six Dirac cross
diagrams not already used above, all associated to the eigenvalues with a
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}
. The reason for this is that we must use up all the
Dirac modes associated to spin-3/2 fields, which is the first component of the spin-3/2 supermultiplet.

All the forty
i\not{D}_{3/2}
cross diagrams in the appendix of
Nilsson:2018lof
must appear a single time each when looking at the entire spectrum. The fact that the spin-1/2 fields
in the spin-3/2 supermultiplets are related to the
i\not{D}_{3/2}
and not
i\not{D}_{1/2}
is a consequence of supersymmetry which uniquely gives this answer.

From the results derived in
Nilsson:1983ru
(or in
Section

4
of this paper) we have

i\not{D}_{1/2}(3,5,7)=-\frac{m}{6}-\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\ \implies\ E_{0}(3/2)=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{5}{6}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}},

(356)

where the first spin-1 field in the supermultiplet is
1^{-}
(see below), and

i\not{D}_{1/2}(4,6,8)=-\frac{m}{6}+\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\ \implies\ E_{0}(3/2)=\frac{3}{2}-\frac{5}{6}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}

(357)

where the first spin-1 field is
1^{+}
(see below). In the notation of
Section

4
these are, respectively, denoted
i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)_{-}}
and
i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)_{+}}
,
see
Figure

3
.

To establish the results for the spin-1 components is here a bit more intricate than for the
2^{+}
multiplets above.
The spin 1 content of these six branches (cross diagrams) is three each of
1^{+}
and
1^{-}
. However, the six
\Delta_{1}
cross diagrams are used twice since the mass square operator has
two square root branches, see
Table

3
. The second occurrence of these six sets of
cross diagrams will appear in the six sets of
1^{-}
supermultiplets discussed below. The two sets of
\pm
signs gives four different sets of

E_{0}
values and all four are needed for everything to work out. To see this we recall the derivation of the
E_{0}(1^{-})
values here.

To obtain the
E_{0}(1^{-})
values we start from
Table

2
,

E_{0}(1^{-})=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{M^{2}/m^{2}+1},

(358)

where
M^{2}
has two branches(see
Table

3
)
36
36

36

Recalling from
Duff:1986hr
that
\Delta_{1}\geq 7m^{2}
we see that
\sqrt{\Delta_{1}+4m^{2}}\geq 3m
.

M^{2}(1^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle(\pm)})=\Delta_{1}+12m^{2}\pm 6m\sqrt{\Delta_{1}+4m^{2}}=(\sqrt{\Delta_{1}+4m^{2}}\pm 3m)^{2}-m^{2},

(359)

where also
\Delta_{1}
has two branches, denoted
\Delta_{1}^{(1)_{\pm}}
in
Equation

132
,

\Delta_{1}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\bigl(20C_{g}+14\pm 2\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\bigr)=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\bigl(\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\pm 1\bigr)^{2}-4m^{2}.

(360)

Inserting the last expression into the one immediately above it gives

M^{2}(1^{-})=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\bigl(\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\pm 1\pm 9\bigr)^{2}-m^{2}.

(361)

The sign choice associated with
\Delta_{1}
is the one that must be correlated to the different cross diagrams as will be done shortly.

The four choices of signs in
Equation

361
give
-10,-8,8,10
to be added to the square root in
M^{2}
. Using
10
and
-10
we
get
37
37

37

Note that
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\geq 10
for all irreps in the relevant Dirac cross diagrams.

E_{0}(1^{-})=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{5}{3}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}},

(362)

while
8
and
-8
give

E_{0}(1^{-})=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{4}{3}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}.

(363)

The first set of
E_{0}(1^{-})
values will be needed in the spin
1^{-}
supermultiplets as demonstrated below while the second set fit into the spin
3/2
ones analysed here.
This last statement follows since the values to be added to
\frac{3}{2}
are
\pm\frac{5}{6}
which arise as
\frac{4}{3}-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{5}{6}
and
-\frac{4}{3}+\frac{1}{2}=-\frac{5}{6}
when relating the different values of
E_{0}
in the multiplet.
We therefore conclude that the content of the six 3/2 supermultiplets is as given above in terms of the numbered Dirac cross diagrams.

Note that the six cross diagrams of
\Delta_{1}
are identical to the
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}
Dirac ones. This is clear from

Figures

2
and
3

which also correlates the signs in their operator
eigenvalues.
38
38

38

The same result is obtained by supersymmetry alone, namely that the
\Delta_{1}
cross diagrams
numbered 1, 3 and 5 in
Nilsson:2018lof
have eigenvalues from the minus branch
and diagrams 2, 4 and 6 from the plus branch.

This assignment of signs must be a consistent choice also in the
1^{-}
supermultiplets
which it is as we will see below.

The
1^{+}
field in this supermultiplet has to come from modes with eigenvalues given by
\Delta_{2}^{(2)_{\pm}}
due to the
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}
.
For these, we have

\Delta_{2}^{(2)_{\pm}}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\Bigl(20C_{g}+44\pm 4\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\Bigr)\ \implies\ E_{0}(1^{+})=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{2}{6}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}.

(364)

Hence, the fields associated to
\Delta_{2}^{(2)_{\pm}}
fit into the supermultiplet containing the spin-3/2 field associated to
i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)_{\mp}}
.
Note that the corresponding cross diagrams in
Figures

3
and
4
agree.

The
i\not{D}_{3/2}
-eigenvalues that should be used for the spin
1/2
components to fit into these six spin
3/2
supermultiplets are (see
Equation

145
)

i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(3)\pm}=\frac{m}{3}\biggl(\frac{1}{2}\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\biggr)\ \implies\ E_{0}(1/2)=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{5}{6}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}},

(365)

where the eigenvalues with a plus and a minus are associated with, respectively, the same cross diagrams as those with eigenvalues
i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)-}
and
i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)+}

(see
Equation

142
and
Figure

3
).

We can summarise the situation for the six spin
3/2
supermultiplets, using the notation of
Section

4
, as follows (ordered as
E_{0},E_{0}-\frac{1}{2},E_{0}+\frac{1}{2},E_{0}
)

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}_{1}\colon\quad\frac{3}{2}(i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)_{-}}),\ 1^{+}(\Delta_{2}^{(2)_{+}}),\ 1^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle(+)}(\Delta_{1}^{(1)_{-}}),\ \frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(3)_{+}}),

(366)

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}_{2}\colon\quad\frac{3}{2}(i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)_{+}}),\ 1^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle(-)}(\Delta_{1}^{(1)_{+}}),\ 1^{+}(\Delta_{2}^{(2)_{-}}),\ \frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(3)_{-}}).

(367)

We emphasise that since for the spin
3/2
operator
i\not{D}_{3/2}
we have no independent way to relate eigenvalues to cross
diagrams (as was done in
Section

4
for
i\not{D}_{1/2}
,
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
) we have here relied entirely on supersymmetry and used the cross diagrams of
Nilsson:2018lof
.

The
1^{-}
supermultiplets

The six sets of
1^{-}
supermultiplets are of the form

D(E_{0},1^{-}:\Delta_{1})\oplus D(E_{0}-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}:i\not{D}_{1/2\,\text{or}\,3/2})\oplus D(E_{0}+\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}:i\not{D}_{3/2\,\text{or}\,1/2})\oplus D(E_{0},0^{-}:Q),

(368)

where
i\not{D}_{1/2}
and
i\not{D}_{3/2}
occur in a similar way to how in the previous case there were three supermultiplets with spin-1 fields coming from
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
.
That this happens here is a direct consequence of supersymmetry and is easily established using the known eigenvalues of the various operators.

The eigenvalues of the
3+3
cross diagrams of
\Delta_{1}
were in this case identified above as the ones coming from 10 and -10 in the expression for
E_{0}(1^{-})
in
Equation

362
. Thus

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}^{(1)_{+}}\colon\ E_{0}(1^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle(+)})=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{5}{3}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}},

(369)

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}^{(1)_{-}}\colon\ E_{0}(1^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle(-)})=\frac{3}{2}-\frac{5}{3}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}},

(370)

where the cross diagrams associated to the two sets of
\Delta_{1}^{(1)\pm}
-eigenvalues are specified in
Figure

2
.

The last component of these supermultiplets is the pseudo-scalar
0^{-}
. Using the
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}
eigenvalues of
Q
in
Equation

139

Q^{(2)\pm}=\frac{m}{3}\bigl(1\pm\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}\bigr),

(371)

and the
0^{-}
mass operator from
Table

3

M^{2}(0^{-})=(Q+3m)^{2}-m^{2},

(372)

we find as expected that all six cross diagrams satisfy

E_{0}(0^{-})=E_{0}(1^{-}).

(373)

Finally, we must check that also the spin-1/2 fields fit into these
1^{-}
supermultiplets.
It is interesting to note that we need to use both
i\not{D}_{1/2}
and
i\not{D}_{3/2}

eigenvalues for this to work.
Let us start with the spin-1/2 energies
E_{0}=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}|M/m|
where the minus sign cannot occur in these supermultiplets due to the unitarity bound
E_{0}(1)\geq 2
.
The mass related to the
i\not{D}_{1/2}
and
i\not{D}_{3/2}
operators are (see
Table

3
,
Equation

142
and
Equation

146
)

\displaystyle M(1/2,i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)_{\pm}})=-i\not{D}_{1/2}-\frac{9m}{2}=-\frac{13m}{3}\mp\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}},

(374)

\displaystyle M(1/2,i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(4)_{\pm}})=i\not{D}_{3/2}+\frac{3m}{2}=\frac{7m}{3}\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}},

(375)

where we should note that it is the second set of
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}
eigenvalues of
i\not{D}_{3/2}
that must be used here. This produces the following two sets of energy
values (for Dirichlet boundary conditions)

\displaystyle E_{0}(1/2,i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)_{\pm}})=\frac{3}{2}\mp\frac{13}{6}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}},

(376)

\displaystyle E_{0}(1/2,i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(4)_{\pm}})=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{7}{6}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+49}}.

(377)

Hence the supermultiplets are (ordered as
(E_{0},E_{0}-1/2,E_{0}+1/2,E_{0})
),
in terms of the notation of
Section

4
,

\displaystyle 1^{-}_{1}\colon\quad 1_{\scriptscriptstyle(+)}^{-}(\Delta_{1}^{(1)_{+}}),\ \frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(4)_{+}}),\ \frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)_{+}}),\ 0^{-}(Q^{(2)_{+}}),

(378)

\displaystyle 1^{-}_{2}\colon\quad 1_{\scriptscriptstyle(-)}^{-}(\Delta_{1}^{(1)_{-}}),\ \frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(2)_{-}}),\ \frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(4)_{-}}),\ 0^{-}(Q^{(2)_{-}}),

(379)

where as before the lower sign in the bracket
1^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle(\pm)}
indicates which branch of
M^{2}
is used.
These results for the cross diagrams imply that there are two short gauge multiplets when
C_{g}=6
, and hence
E_{0}=2
, connected to the
two adjoint irreps
(2,0;0)
and
(0,0;2)
of the isometry group
Sp_{2}\times Sp^{C}_{1}
.

The
1^{+}
supermultiplets

So far,
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry, together with the operator eigenvalues derived in previous works and the result for the spin-3/2 operator obtained in
Karlsson:2021oxd
,
have been sufficient to uniquely fix all relations between operator eigenvalues and isometry irreps (that is, cross diagrams). This nice state of affairs will no longer
persist when we now turn to the eight spin
1^{+}
supermultiplets, each with the content

D(E_{0},1^{+}:\Delta_{2})\oplus D(E_{0}-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}:i\not{D}_{3/2})\oplus D(E_{0}+\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}:i\not{D}_{3/2})\oplus D(E_{0},0^{+}:\Delta_{L}),

(380)

where the potential ambiguity for the last entry, the
0^{+}
scalars, which could be given by either
\Delta_{0}
or
\Delta_{L}
is settled by supersymmetry.

It is clear that the remaining eight cross diagrams for
\Delta_{2}
in
Figure

4

account for the
{\bf 3}
and
{\bf 5}
spin
1^{+}
components of these supermultiplets.
These must therefore all be associated with the eigenvalues
Equation

135
and energies

\Delta_{2}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,20C_{g}\quad\implies\quad E_{0}(1^{+},\Delta_{2})=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+9}}.

(381)

Now we understand that the spin-1/2 fields
must be associated with
i\not{D}_{3/2}
since the eigenvalues of
i\not{D}_{1/2}
do not contain
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+9}}
.

Similarly, the scalar fields
0^{+}
are related to
\Delta_{L}
-eigenvalues
Equation

136
and the energies

\Delta_{L}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,(20C_{g}+36)\quad\implies\quad E_{0}(0^{+},\Delta_{L})=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+9}}.

(382)

The unexpected feature that appears here is that neither of the two sets of eigenvalues, related to the
{\bf 3}
and
{\bf 5}
cross diagrams, have a negative square root branch (cf.
\Delta_{2}^{(2)_{\pm}}
). This means that
there is a degeneracy in the spectrum since different modes in the same isometry irrep must pairwise be associated with the same operator
eigenvalue. This must happen for all the fields in these
1^{+}
supermultiplets whose irreps are of the kind
(p,q;r)
with either
r=p
or
r=p\pm 2
.
As we will discover below when discussing the Wess–Zumino supermultiplets,
the ones containing scalar fields related to
\Delta_{L}
, and only these ones, will display the same phenomenon.

This being concluded here, we should now recall the results of
Section

4
on the
\Delta_{2}
eigenmodes and their eigenvalues which support this conclusion.
In fact, in
Section

4
we identified explicitly the different mode functions that have the same
\Delta_{2}
-eigenvalues,
\Delta_{2}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,20C_{g}
, and the modes are precisely the ones
in the
{\bm{3}}
and
{\bm{5}}
cross diagrams given above. The notation used in
Section

4
to make this clear was to denote eigenvalues associated with the mode functions (and thus the cross diagrams)
related to
{\bm{3}}
by

\Delta_{2}^{(3)}
while those related to
{\bm{5}}
was denoted
\Delta_{2}^{(3)^{\prime}}
despite the fact that their eigenvalues (and masses and
E_{0}
) are the same.
Unfortunately, why this happens is not clear from the results we have obtained so far. We mention here the fact (which is further discussed in the Conclusions,
Section

6
)
that this degeneracy occurs only in supermultiplets related to modes of
\Delta_{L}
.
39
39

39

However, not in all supermultiplets containing
\Delta_{L}
since the ones in
{\bf 5}
with
r=p\pm 4
are not degenerate.

To conclude the discussion of the
1^{+}
multiplets we need also the spin-3/2 eigenvalues

i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(2)_{\pm}}=-\frac{m}{2}\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+9}}\ \implies\ E_{0}(1/2,i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(2)_{\pm}})=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+9}},

(383)

which completes the two
1^{+}
lines in
Table

4
. The relevant cross diagrams are the same for all four entries in each line
1^{+}
in
Table

4
,
and coincide with those specified in
Figure

4
.
It is now clear that there are, as expected, no short multiplets in this case.

The situation for spin
1^{+}
can be summarised as follows:

\displaystyle 1^{+}_{1}\colon\quad 1^{+}(\Delta_{2}^{(3)}),\ \frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(2)_{-}}),\ \frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(2)_{+}}),\ 0^{+}(\Delta_{L}^{(1)}),

(384)

\displaystyle 1^{+}_{2}\colon\quad 1^{+}(\Delta_{2}^{(3)^{\prime}}),\ \frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(2)^{\prime}_{-}}),\ \frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(2)^{\prime}_{+}}),\ 0^{+}(\Delta_{L}^{(1)^{\prime}}),

(385)

where we emphasise again that we in
Section

4
have derived the eigenvalues for the cross diagrams of
{\bm{3}}
and
{\bm{5}}
and found them to coincide, hence the notation

\Delta_{2}^{(3)}
and
\Delta_{2}^{(3)^{\prime}}
.
For the rest of the spins, on the other hand, the corresponding mode calculations have not been done but it follows from supersymmetry that their cross diagrams are identical to the
\bm{3}\oplus\bm{5}
ones of
\Delta_{2}^{(3),(3)^{\prime}}
.

The Wess–Zumino supermultiplets

There are 14 sets of Wess–Zumino supermultiplets (see
Equation

222
) of which two sets must be related to the remaining modes of the Dirac operator with eigenvalues
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}
. This fits nicely
with the fact that also the two
M_{\scriptscriptstyle(\pm)}^{2}(0^{+})
branches of scalars connected to
\Delta_{0}
are left to account for. This explains the first two lines, denoted WZ1 and WZ2, of

Table

5
.

Thus the WZ1 and WZ2 multiplets have
E_{0}
and cross diagrams governed by the spin-1/2 fields. These have

M(1/2)=-i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(1)_{\pm}}-\frac{9m}{2}
with
i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(1)_{\pm}}=\frac{m}{2}\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}
(see
Table

3
and
Equation

141
)
where the modes for the upper sign fills a whole cross diagram and similarly for the modes with the lower sign except that the singlet is missing, see
Figure

3
.

The
E_{0}
spectrum, valid for the upper sign on
i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(1)_{\pm}}
and for all
C_{g}\geq 0
, is

E_{0}(1/2,i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(1)_{+}})=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}|M/m|=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{5}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},

(386)

For the lower sign the situation is slightly different due to the absolut value on the mass in
E_{0}
. Now
C_{g}>0
but
|M|=\frac{m}{3}|-15\mp\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}|
depends on the relative size of the two terms under the absolut sign. We get the following two cases

\displaystyle E_{0}(1/2,i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(1)_{-}})=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}|M/m|=\frac{3}{2}-\frac{5}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},\quad

\displaystyle C_{g}\geq\frac{36}{5},

(387)

\displaystyle E_{0}(1/2,i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(1)_{-}})=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}|M/m|=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{5}{2}-\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},\quad

\displaystyle C_{g}<\frac{36}{5},

(388)

where the limit in the last case is only satisfied by the two values
C_{g}=4,\frac{19}{4}
(recall that the spectrum here involves only irreps
(p,q;p)
).
Note that we have here used only the upper (Dirichlet) sign in
E_{0}(1/2)=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}|M/m|
. The other case, related to Neumann boundary conditions, is studied separately in
Section

5.1
for the five possible values of
C_{g}
allowed by unitarity
(which occur only for the lower sign in
i\not{D}_{1/2}^{(1)_{\pm}}
and not necessarily for all the members of the supermultiplets). Further details on the cases allowing for Neumann boundary conditions are given in
Section

5.2
.

It is now interesting to compare the situation above for the spin-1/2 fields with the one for the scalars. From
Table

2
and
Table

3
we see that
\Delta_{0}^{(1)}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}20C_{g}
gives
a dependence of
M^{2}(0^{+})
on the absolut value of
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}\pm 18
. This gives, for the plus branch (and for Dirichlet boundary conditions),

E_{0}(0^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle(+)})=\frac{3}{2}+3+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},

(389)

while for the minus branch we again find two cases

\displaystyle E_{0}(0^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle(-)})=\frac{3}{2}-3+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},\quad C_{g}\geq\frac{243}{20},

(390)

\displaystyle E_{0}(0^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle(-)})=\frac{3}{2}+3-\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},\quad C_{g}<\frac{243}{20},

(391)

which contains the five values listed in
Table

6
, that is,
C_{g}=4,\frac{19}{4},\frac{39}{4},10,12
.
Here the signs in the bracket on
0^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle(\pm)}
refer to the two branches of
M^{2}(0^{+})
in
Table

3
.

This also fits with the
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}
branch of eigenvalues for
Q
(see
Equation

140
and
Table

3
)

Q^{(3)_{\pm}}=m\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}\ \implies\ M^{2}(Q^{(3)_{\pm}})=\Bigl(4m\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}\Bigr)^{2}-m^{2},

(392)

and hence
E_{0}
depends on the absolut value of
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}\pm 12
. This gives

E_{0}
values (for Dirichlet boundary conditions), noting that
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}>12
for all irreps in WZ2,

E_{0}(0^{-},Q^{(3)_{\pm}})=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(M/m)^{2}+1}=\frac{3}{2}\pm 2+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}}.

(393)

Again the lower sign in
E_{0}
is not discussed here and the reader is referred to
Section

5.1
for more details.

These results lead to the two Wess–Zumino supermultiplets WZ1 and WZ2 in the Wess–Zumino table for
C_{g}>0
in
Section

5.1
which turn out to have a slightly different
structure (note the order of the fields
(E_{0},E_{0}-\frac{1}{2},E_{0}+\frac{1}{2}))
:

\displaystyle\text{WZ1}_{C_{g}\geq 0}\colon\quad

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}^{(1)_{+}}_{1/2}),\ 0^{-}(Q^{(3)_{+}}),\ 0_{\scriptscriptstyle(+)}^{+}(\Delta_{0}^{(1)}),\quad

\displaystyle E_{0}=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{5}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},

(394)

\displaystyle\text{WZ2}_{C_{g}\geq\frac{243}{20}}\colon\quad

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}^{(1)_{-}}_{1/2}),\ 0^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle(-)}(\Delta_{0}^{(1)}),\ 0^{-}(Q^{(3)_{-}}),\quad

\displaystyle E_{0}=\frac{3}{2}-\frac{5}{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+81}},

(395)

plus the special cases listed above to complete WZ2 for
0<C_{g}<\frac{243}{20}
. We note here that without involving Neumann boundary conditions there are no
Wess–Zumino supermultiplets with
C_{g}=4,\frac{19}{4},\frac{39}{4},10,12
. These five cases are studied in detail in
Section

5.1
.

The
Q
cross diagrams relevant for fields in WZ1 and WZ2 can be found in the appendix of
Nilsson:2018lof
.
40
40

40

The cross diagrams in the appendix of
Nilsson:2018lof
are related by
[i\not{D}_{1/2}](1)=[\Delta_{0}](1)=[Q](1)
,
[i\not{D}_{1/2}](2)=[\Delta_{0}](1)-(0,0;0)=[Q](6+7-5)
.

By supersymmetry, they have to be identical to the
i\not{D}^{(1)_{\pm}}_{1/2}
cross diagrams in
Figure

3
.
Note that the singlet irrep
(0,0;0)
should be removed from the irrep spectrum of
0^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle(-)}(\Delta_{0}^{(1)})
even though it appears in the cross diagram of
\Delta_{0}^{(1)}
, as explained in
Duff:1986hr
, which is consistent with the cross diagrams of
i\not{D}^{(1)_{\pm}}_{1/2}
.
One should note that there is only one mode with
C_{g}=0
here, and that this mode belongs to the multiplet WZ1. The multiplet WZ2 lacks
the
C_{g}=0
mode which it must to be consistent with unitarity. The
C_{g}=0
mode is discussed further in
Section

5.1
.

The final twelve sets of Wess–Zumino multiplets are in some sense less intriguing since the only special
case is
C_{g}=0
(see
Nilsson:2023ctq
).
41
41

41

Note that in all three expressions for the mass discussed here the square root term is larger then the term without a square root when
C_{g}>0
.

Leaving this case for a separate discussion we now explain the rows WZ3–6 in
Table

5
. Checking which operator eigenvalues that have not been used so far we
find that they are all of the
\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}
form and occur only for
\Delta_{L}
,
Q
and
i\not{D}_{3/2}
. The remaining cross diagrams in
Nilsson:2018lof
are also
precisely the ones that are needed to form the Wess–Zumino supermultiplets denoted WZ3–6 in
Table

5
.
From the results of
Nilsson:2018lof
we conclude that these should be grouped into two
Sp_{1}^{C}
sets of
{\bf 1}+{\bf 5}
. To verify this we need the eigenvalues for these three operators

\Delta_{L}
,
Q
and
i\not{D}_{3/2}
. We start with the spin-1/2 ones (see
Equation

143
and
Table

3
)

i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(1)_{\pm}}=\frac{m}{6}\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\ \implies\ M(1/2)=i\not{D}_{3/2}+\frac{3m}{2}=4m\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}

(396)

which give the spin-1/2
E_{0}
values in all the WZ3–6 multiplets as

E_{0}(1/2)=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{2}|M/m|=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{5}{6}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}},

(397)

where only the sign for Dirichlet boundary conditions is used.
One can check that Neumann boundary conditions are impossible due to
unitarity.
42
42

42

Recall that Neumann boundary conditions are related to irreps with
E_{0}\leq\frac{3}{2}
.

The eigenvalues of the modes associated with the scalar fields are determined by
Equation

137

\Delta_{L}=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,\bigl(20C_{g}+32\pm 4\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\bigl)=\frac{m^{2}}{9}\,\bigl(\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\pm 2\bigl)^{2}+3m^{2},

(398)

and with
M^{2}=\Delta_{L}-4m^{2}

Table

3
we find

E_{0}(0^{+},\Delta_{L}^{(2)_{\pm}})=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}},

(399)

and the ones of the pseudo-scalars (see
Equation

138
and
Table

3
)

Q^{(1)_{\pm}}=-\frac{m}{3}\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\ \implies\ M^{2}(0^{-})=\biggl(\frac{8m}{3}\pm\frac{m}{3}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}\biggl)^{2}-m^{2},

(400)

which leads to

E_{0}(0^{-})=\frac{3}{2}\pm\frac{4}{3}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}.

(401)

For the scalars
0^{\pm}
Neumann boundary conditions are possible for the minus branch in their expressions for
E_{0}
above but since Neumann boundary conditions
are impossible for the spin-1/2 fields, as we saw above, supersymmetry implies that this is the case also for the scalars.
A full analysis of possible Neumann boundary conditions is carried out in
Section

5.2
, then for both the left- and right-squashed vacua.

The above facts clearly give rise to
C_{g}>0
Wess–Zumino supermultiplets of the following two kinds (ordered as
(E_{0},E_{0}-\frac{1}{2},E_{0}+\frac{1}{2})
)

\displaystyle\text{WZ3--4}\colon\quad\frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(1)_{+}}),\ 0^{+}(\Delta_{L}^{(2)_{+}}),\ 0^{-}(Q^{(1)_{+}}),\quad E_{0}=\frac{3}{2}+\frac{5}{6}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}},

(402)

\displaystyle\text{WZ5--6}:\quad\frac{1}{2}(i\not{D}_{3/2}^{(1)_{-}}),\ 0^{-}(Q^{(1)_{-}}),\ 0^{+}(\Delta_{L}^{(2)_{-}}),\quad E_{0}=\frac{3}{2}-\frac{5}{6}+\frac{1}{6}\sqrt{\vphantom{C_{G}}\smash[b]{20C_{g}+1}}.

(403)

The remaining cross diagrams cannot, with the available information from
Nilsson:1983ru
;
Nilsson:2018lof
;
Ekhammar:2021gsg
;
Karlsson:2021oxd
;
Nilsson:2023ctq
and this paper, be associated with WZ3–4 or WZ5–6 in a unique way
so we have put all their irreps into one coloured cross diagram in
Figure

8
.
However, from the lists of cross diagrams in the
appendix of
Nilsson:2018lof
it seems clear that
each pair, i.e., WZ3–4 and WZ5–6, must be connected to
{\bm{1}}+{\bm{5}}
worth of cross diagrams.
This completes the explanation of all the entries
in the Wess–Zumino table for
C_{g}>0
.

The analysis of the Wess–Zumino multiplets with
C_{g}=0
is carried out in detail in
Section

5.1
, following closely
Nilsson:2023ctq
, and is hence not discussed here.

As should now be clear, using the results obtained in this section all supermultiplets appearing in the left-squashed
S^{7}
spectrum (listed in the beginning of
Section

2.1
and
Section

5.1
),
except the twelve Wess–Zumino multiplets that involve the Lichnerowicz operator, can be uniquely associated with operator eigenvalues and groups of cross diagrams
from the appendix of
Nilsson:2018lof
. Thus it is possible to decide exactly which cross diagrams these twelve Wess–Zumino multiplets
must be connected to in order that all the cross diagrams are used in a consistent way. However, to be able to divide these cross diagrams in two groups and associate them with
the two branches of
E_{0}
values the information in this paper is not enough. Fortunately, using input from
Duboeuf:2022mam
this can be done, i.e., the results of
Duboeuf:2022mam
allows us to split
Figure

8
(which gives the isometry irrep content of WZ3–4 and WZ5–6 combined) into the two rows in
Figure

5
.

All isometry irreps of the twelve Wess–Zumino supermultiplet towers WZ3–6 (including the Page supermultiplet), corresponding to two sets of
